Carlos and Maria Mendez both work 
at home — and are heavy Internet 
users. But until they installed a 
SOHOware home network, they 
had to take turns getting on the Web, 
which proved to be a real problem 
when they both had deadlines. 

Now, thanks to a SOHOware net- 


work — and a broadband high-speed 


Internet connection from their local 2 
a 
cable company — they are totally up ‘a 


to speed when it comes to the Web. 
They not only get super fast Internet 
access, but since both computers are 
networked, Carlos and Maria can be 
on the Web at the same time. 

Of course, there are a lot of other 
advantages to the home network, 
too. They no longer have to carry 
floppy disks from one PC to another 
every time they need to copy a file or 


print a document. Instead both PCs

EDITOR'S NOTE 


JULY 2000 


LETTERS TO THE EDITOR: WRITE TO BACKCHAT@ZIFFDAVIS.COM 


Solomon and Metallica 


Y NOW, YOU'VE READ THE ARGUMENTS PRO AND 

con regarding the proposed Microsoft breakup. 

We'll leave the shouting over its economic and 

corporate implications to our business-magazine 

brethren. Here, we think mostly about what things 
mean to the everyday Net user. And despite the scenar- 
ios of doom, turmoil, or radical change we've read on 
both sides of the split’em-up question, let me suggest 
my radical idea: No matter which way it’s resolved, most 
of us aren't going to notice much difference at all. 

Personally, I’m not a 
major fan of Windows. If 
the “operating system” of 
any other modern tech- 
nology—radio, TV, 
stereo, movies—were as 
complicated for the aver- 
age user, we would have 
gone nuts years ago. I’ve 
written before about 
what the world might be 
like if it operated by 
today’s computer and 
Internetrules: lightbulbs 
thatneed to be booted up, toasters thatrequire plug-ins, 
cars that freeze in mid-motion, TV programs the size of 
postage stamps. And Istill don’t get, [really don’t get, why 
we can’t have complex computers that turn on instant- 
ly. Orasystem that installs new programs without hav- 
ing to restart. Or software (as a novice user asked me 
aboutrecently) that hasa brighter idea than putting the 
Shut Down button behind Start. 

I can’t see how, despite the courtroom drama, the 
Solomon-like solution of cutting Microsoft in two is 
going to affect much of this. (An operating-system 
monopoly is still a monopoly—on PCs anyway.) I’m 
still going to be waiting for the damned thing to reboot, 
and so are you. Meanwhile, remembering what it was 
like before Windows, in the dear departed days of DOS, 
I'm grateful for what little simplicity we have. 

Anyway, it’s going to take years until the lawyers on 
this Dickensian case run out of things to bill for, so Isus- 
pect the world will move on and make a lot of this 
largely irrelevant. User-friendliness will come not from 
one or two Microsofts but from innovation out there in 
the new Netterritories. (Already, some worry more about 


BLEEDING ME: Metallica sued Napster, then took names of 
users the band said violated its copyright by “sharing” songs. 


bigger monopolies emerging out of AOL Time Warner, 
or even out of our licensing partner, Yahoo! See “Cut & 
Paste,” page 71.) Whether Bill Gates is $50 billion rich- 
eror poorer, by the time the final gavel falls we'll be deal- 
ing with changes so purely Net-driven that we may be 
saying, “Operating system? Browser? Whazzat?” 

Almostas much in the newsare the wars over music 
copyrights, with every day bringing fresh cannonades 
from the trenches: MP3.com—boom! Napster—boing! 
Metallica—clang! And during a war, it’s the deeply 
human stories that we 
remember. You will too, 
when you read the per- 
sonal account by associ- 
ate online editor JOSH 
ROBERTSON, who 
reveals his musical tres- 
passesin “Confessions of 
a Napster Fanatic.” You'll 
laugh, you'll cry, you'll 
wonder if he'll be ar- 
rested. (We’ll stand 
behind his method of 
“participatory report- 
ing”: He shared in the MP3 experience in order to write 
about it.) And as he assures us, he didn’t download any 
Metallica tunes. It’s also a great warm-up for our music 
issue next month. 

On a more practical level, this is the month we roll 
out our “50 Most Incredibly Useful Sites.” As glitzy and 
ditzy as all this Internet change can be, sometimes you 
just want to say, “It’s the usefulness, stupid.” Our very 
useful team of contributing writer RICHARD BUTNER 
and senior editor GORDON BASS says it for us. You'll 
also want to check out the budding entertainment 
moguls at Pseudo studios in “Creating the New Boob 
Tube,” by CHARLES PLATT. Then top it off with asso- 
ciate editor JEREMY CAPLAN's “Beg forIt!,” where you'll 
discover that if you can’t bea rich geek, you can always 
be an online panhandler. And every once in a while 
(checked yourstock quotes lately?) the two become one. 


Be Cir 


Barry Golson, Editor in Chief

prepared working lecture notes

room are not the same docu-

dents’ notes threatens to become 
a viable substitute for atten-

EBERT VS. E-BOOKS 
Regarding Roger Ebert's May 
column [page 72]: Mr. Ebert, if 
you refuse to read e-books, you 
may soon discover you have 
little of quality to read. Most 
people who declare they will 
never read e-books do so under 
the false assumption that all 
books worthy of being read 
appear in printed form. 


This is no longer the case. } 


Many publishers have been 


gobbled up by giant corpora- } 


tions that are interested only 
in the bottom line. In the last 


have watched dif- 
ferent kinds of genre novels 
disappear off bookstore 
shelves because the numbers 
sold aren't large enough. 
Poetry, earlier literature like 
the Melville ard James you 
mention, new literary fiction, 


few years, I 


! and nonfiction not aimed at a 


general audience are also begin 
ning to vanish. However, many 
of them are reappearing as 
e-books. Readers who love 
these books are creating their 
own electronic publishing 
companies to keep themalive. 
Writers are selling to these 
e-publishers because they are 
allowed the freedom to write 
books of the heart, not books 
by marketing committee. 
—Marilynn Byerly: 
@aol.com 


CONTENT IS KING 
[ enjoy your publication and 
Roger Ebert’s columns. But I 


have to comment on his criti 
cism of the e-book asa concept. 
[think that his prediction that 
the entire e-book industry is 
going to fall on its face is a bit 
premature. 

The most important part of 
the reading experience is what 
it does for the life of the mind. 
Reading in an electronic format 
is still reading. The texts stored 
and presented in the reader 
software that I have chosen to 
install on my laptop look exact 
y like books, and their words 
are the same as those in the 
hard-copy versions. 

There definitely are prob 
ems associated with electron 


ic publishing, but Ebert has 
: overlooked theimportant ones. 
i Isn’t he concerned about the 
i fact that some electronic pub 


ishers have removed editors 
from the process? Editors have 
played a vital role in modern 
publishing, and there is no way

believe, online voting was no 
a huge success here in Arizona

than 800 of us Mac users who 
were not able to vote in the 


with many e-mail contacts to 
the local Democratic Party 
office in Phoenix and back East 
to the company conducting

to help, although Election.com

be no problem with my com-

A Quiet Influence 


Godfrey Reggio’s films aren’t as pop 


ular as Spielberg’s. But his vision is 


as ubiquitous in media as the latest rock video 


fg HE CUTTING-EDGE FILMS OF DIRECTOR GODFREY 


Reggio are not what you'd call blockbusters. 
However, if you've ever watched a music video, 
it’s likely you've seen the influence of Koy- 
aanisqatsi or one of his other cinematic works. 


NETBRIEFS.07/00 | 


With Nagoygatsi, Reggio’s newest project, the 
monk-cum-director takes his signature long-view 
approach to technology and the Net: “We're tran- 
siting into another [form of] nature, into a techno- 
logical environment....It’s not inconceivable that 
we're going into a posthuman context.” 

Nagqoygatsi is the final piece of the Qatsi trilogy, 
which continues the messages of Reggio’s 1983 
debut, Koyaanisqatsi (Hopi for “life out of balance”), 
and 1988's Powaqgatsi (“life in transformation”). The 
imagery of these films—buildings toppling, people 
cramming into elevators—has been reflected in 
vehicles as diverse as Van Halen videos and films 
such as Kundun and Armageddon. 

The official Reggio Web site KOYAANISQATSI 
[qatsi.org] hosts message boards where youcan pon- 
der the notions of cultural and technological trans- 
formation in his films—from environmental threats 
to the Challenger disaster. It also solicits production 
funds. (After all, this isn’t Hollywood.) 

Reggio plans to use the Net for more than just 
promotion. “We intend to involve [it] in the very 
production of the film itself,” he says. “We're look- 
ing in the areas of sound, music, and image to invite 
participation ona vast scale through the Internet.” 

Given Reggio’s ambivalence about technology, 
there’s irony in these Net links. But to him, it makes 
sense: “What better tool to use than the globalizing 
tool itself?... That’s the impure path that one has to 
follow to be living in this world.” —B.E.

I'm thinking of redecorating my bathroom. 


Fortunately, picking anew decor for my house in cyBeRTOWN 


necessary CityCash. I just wonder 

if I need new towels now too. 
| This is the sublime and mun 
dane experience of residency ina 
do-it-yourself digital town. Over 
the past year, Cybertown has 
lured nearly 300,000 so-called 
immigrants to its dense 3-D com 
munity. Every day, members from 
Italy to Long Island are slipping 
into custom avatars to roam the 
town plaza, feed their pets, fur- 
nish their homes, and—to pay for 
itall—earn CityCash by working. 
Those avatars, by the way, aren't 


46 JuLyY 2000 


A- Transpar: + 


[cybertown.com], a sprawling Web metropolis, doesn’t 
involve schlepping to Home Depot. Instead, I stroll 
through Cybertown’s flea market, where I find a hot- 


pink commode onsale. Groovy, I think, handing over the 


the cartoony characters you'll find 
in graphical chat worlds such as 
the Palace (see Webville, Septem 
ber ’99), where surfers chat in 
what's essentially an online cos- 
tume party. Using Avatar Studio, a 
free downloadable software pro 
gram, you can design your Cyber- 
town persona, from height and 
skin tone to nose length and 
wardrobe. You can even drop in 
your own face shot and get that 
long overdue cheek-and-jowl job. 

The five-year-old site was until 
about a year ago somewhat of a

are infinitely adjustable to your liking. 


geek subculture. Now, with back 
ing from Intel and BLAXXUN

gone mainstream. 

Cybertown citizens always have 
aplace to call home. Click tomove 
into one ofits enclaves; click again 
to choose your home. Then spend 
your CityCash on furnishings to 
keep up with the virtual Joneses. 

During my first tour, I visited 
the lavish home of 16-year-old 
Paolo. “I just can’t get enough of 
the people here,” said my host, rep 
resented by a two-headed rag doll 
avatar. He then excused himself 
to hunt for a new sofa. 

Maybe reality-based games like 
the Sims have it right. People don’t 
just want to fight dragons; they 
want to accessorize.

| By GETPADD! vo mane 


how much you love owning 
your own business, billing clients is 
probably one of your least pleasant 
tasks. End the tedium by using the 
Time Bills service at OPENAIR.COM, a 
site that serves the day-to-day 
accounting needs of entrepreneurs and 
small businesses. Begin by keeping a 
log of your labors on the site. When it's 
time to collect, the service crunches the 
numbers into a report of billable hours 
and generates e-mail invoices. If the 
idea of an e-bill doesn't sit well with 
your customers, OpenAir will print a 
paper invoice and send it via snail mail 


for $1.65. Jc,

_SAVETIME Are a working woman's 
financial concerns really that different 
from a man’s? The publishers of 
MSMONEY.COM think so. Operating 
on the premise that the average female 
lives longer and earns less than the 
average male, the site provides the 
tools that women need to manage their 
money and plan for retirement. In 
addition to offering investment, 
banking, and mortgage services, 
the site hosts 
forums in 
which mem- 
| bers can chat 
and compare 
notes on finan- 
cial strategies. 
This newcom- 
er's emphasis 
on building a 
gender-specific 
community sets it— 
anda sprinkling of other emerging sites 
for women—apart from the crowd. —J.C.

Can’t get it together? Try some virtual 
coaching and a swift motivational kick 


ERSONAL FITNESS 
trainers? Out. Per- 
sonal life coaches? 

In. Thanks to 
COACH U [coachu.com], a 
fleet of inspirational mentors 
is waiting to help you climb 
the corporate ladder, attack 
your spring cleaning, write 
that long-postponed musical 
about the lost Donner Party, or 
carve out more time for the 
kids. “Students” seeking wis- 
dom visit the site to finda 
coach near them who special 
izes in their particular needs. 
Coaches combine the best 
aspects of a motivational men- 
tor and a nagging parent. Tell 
the coach your goals; he'll tell 
you how torealize them, riding 
herd until the project is com- 
pleted or the goal is reached. 
Coaching, usually conducted 


SAVETIME 
SAVEMONEY 


by phone, costs $200 to 
$500 per month fora 
package of four weekly 
30-minute sessions. 

If your goal is to switch 
careers, Coach U may be able to 
help, since it serves as a virtu- 
al school for personal coaches. 
Since its inception in 1994, the 
company has schooled 4,000 
coaches and students, with 
about 2,000 newbies matricu- 
lating in the next year. Coach U 
makes good use of the Web in 
the training process, providing 
online visuals to accompany 
lectures for many of the 36 
classes (total tuition: $3,495) 
required for certification. 

Do you need a coach? Only 
you can answer that. But per- 
hapsyou havea friend who can 
motivate you to take this 
important step. —L.G.

DEAR GASTON, 


Recently, an online 
acquaintance told me 
that my favorite actress 
(an award winner) was 
visiting incognito in the

‘Audible

Robin Williams enters 
| the virtual talk show space 


E HAS BEEN CAST AS AN ALIEN, A 
woman, and a sentient robot. But 
Robin Williams's present real-life 
role as the host of an online talk 


| show may prove to be his toughest. 
Since April, Williams has been presiding 
over a series of chats at AUDIBLE.COM, a 
4 site distributing on-demand, spoken-word | 


news, audio books, and entertainment. 
Williams offers weekly comedy rou- | 

tines and conversations with fellow 

celebs. The show's eclectic guest book— 

which has included former New York 


culled from the comic's | WILLIAMS, VISIT 
personal Rolodex. “I start- | 

ed off with friends,” he 

says, “because they [know] 

that I'm not going to blindside them with 
some strange question like, ‘What about 
you and the chicken?’” 

But rest assured, that doesn't mean he's 
dishing out bland, family-friendly fare here. 
“Since censorship is thus far negligible, 
you can put up a warning and you're pretty 
much open to anything,” he says. And to 
Williams, “anything” includes some new | 
names. “I'd love to introduce some of the 
street performers or comedy performers 
who couldn't get a venue without this.” 

While Williams scouts out new talent, 
he's also surveying Audible’s vast collec- 
tion of recordings. “They've got great com- 
edy, archival stuff from [George] Carlin 
and all sorts of things....If my show helps 
bring spoken word back, it'd be great to 
hear people [saying], ‘Wait a minute, this 
stuff is quite fascinating.’” —BE.

and I had sparred in this magazine [Janu- 
ary '00] over whether HateWatch was 
indeed fighting bigotry on the Web or sim- 
ply providing a convenient click-through 
listing of hate sites. The debate was not 
fiery. We both found ourselves bemused 
by how the nature of the Web creates puz- 
zles: Is what you intend to do on the Web 
what you end up doing, or have you been 
sucked down a McLuhanesque wormhole? 
Which brings us back to Pornutopia. A 
show of hands indicated that about 50 
| audience members perused pornog- 
raphy, three never did, and about 
150 weren't telling. Most of us 
assumed that pornography 
appealed only to men. 
Not so, said my fel- 
low columnist 
Donna Lafram- 
boise, of Canada’s 
National Post; there 
are best-selling sub- 
| genres of romance 
novels, read only by 
women, thatinclude 
sex in startling and 
kinky detail. Oddly, 
when the Canadian

every body 


Porn sites can be a plague, but they also 
celebrate diversity— of a kind 


| HE NAME OF THE PANEL WAS “PORNUTOPIA.” NOBODY KNEW WHAT 
it meant. That was typical of the Conference on World Affairs, 
which is held every April at the University of Colorado and gath- 
ers 100 people to appear on panels about every subject imagin- 
able. m I've been attending the Boulder conference forever. This 
year, Y-Life editor in chief Barry Golson joined me to chaira debate I had 
with David Goldman, founder of HATEWATCH [hatewatch.org]. Goldman 


government started censoring American 
pornography for men, it ignored the 
women’s market. Canadian women can 
read all the porn they want. Men can read 
ittoo, although they might not enjoy being 
depicted as mindless sex objects. 

On the other hand, maybe some would. 
The panel became a discussion of how the 
Web creates support groups for every sex- 
ual taste. We decried child pornography 
(none of us had ever seen it on the Web; 
then again, we weren't looking for it). But 

aftera woman stood up andattacked 
porn for creating an 
image of femininity 
soimprobably per- 
fect that no real 
woman could ever 
hope to measure up, 
we learned from 
the responses that 
extremely real 
women are more 
popular in Web- 
based eroticism 
than you might 
imagine. 
If you download 
the listings of all the 


Usenet sex groups, you do find those devot- 
ed to Playmates and Pets and Pamela Ander- 
son Lee. But youalso find groups treasuring 
older women, plump women, Amazonian 
women, small women, women wearing 
glasses,and women with large breasts, small 
breasts, saggy breasts. Go to the Web cam 
sites and you'll discover lots of women who 
apparently are satisfying exhibitionist ten- 
dencies by Webcasting video of themselves 
nude (often from the neck down, objectify- 
ing themselves ina way that Playboy would 
never dare to do). Check out the Yahoo! 
photo clubs and you'll find as many devot- 
ed to BBWs (big, beautiful women)as to con- 
ventional sex symbols. Andthe Webis thor- 
oughly integrated, with all racial and ethnic 
groups cheek, so to speak, by jowl. 

What interests me is how democratic 
(NOS iG cbqes 
Rejecting the nar- / 
row standards of | 


| 
female beauty, |

extremely real 
they by the thou- | women are more 
sands celebrate | popular in Web- 
ordinary, real, no | based eroticism

Consider NormaStitz (not, obviously, her 


real name). She is a fortyish African- | 


American woman whose site proclaims 
that she is the Guinness record holder for 
the world’s largest breasts. On a chatty, 
homemade Website [normastitz.com], she 
features herself and several “friends” who 
are all clearly runners-up for the Guinness 
crown. There is no intercourse on hersite— 
just acres of pulchritude. She makes money 
by selling videos and by charging for access 
to the members’ section. It is clear that this 
isa cottage industry for the self-employed 
Norma. She may not conform to conven- 
tional standards of female beauty. But she 
has asweet smile and believes that if you've 
got it, flaunt it. 

1 doubt that the Pornutopia discussion 
changed the mind of anyone, pro or con. 
But the audience seemed surprised and even 
pleased that the Web, far from promoting 
the narrow, mainstream definitions of 
female beauty, celebrates diversity. Differ- 
ent strokes for different folks. a

um Copyright Act, a 
institutions liable if 


quietly signed the bil 


nthe Digital Age, 
who owns ideas? 


The Net mandates new thinking aboul law, 


commerce, technology, and culture 


EARLY TWO YEARS AGO, CONGRESS PASSED THE DIGITAL MILLENNI- 
aw that makes individuals, Web sites, and 
hey don’t take steps to get rid of software 
that is used to transmit copyrighted material. President Clinton 
,and even in the hypersensitive Net media 
universe, nobody paid much attention to it. That’s changed. m Inrecent 


months, the DMCA has been cited as the basis for numerous actions by 


the music and movie industries trying to 
reclaim the notion—severely challenged 
by the Internet—that ideas and cultural 
creations can still be owned in traditional 
ways, that royalties can still be collected in 
the age of MP3 players. 

This question becomes more timely by 
the day. But property rights have never 
been absolute or static. All property can be 
subordinated to other interests at some 
point. Your car can’t 
be driven at any 
speed over your 
neighbor's lawn; 
your dogs can be 
seized by the local 
humane society if 
you mistreat them. 
Now the Net and 
the Web have 
taken the debate 
about copyright 
and intellectual 
property toa 
completely dif- 
ferent level. No- 
body can clearly define what these things 
mean in virtual space, let alone how they 
should be regulated or policed. 


Younger Americans in particular have 
grown up assembling vast, personalized 
archives of contemporary culture—Web 
site bookmarks, music, movies, biogra- 
phies and home pages, favorite pictures, 
articles, and texts. Many of these users have 
no direct experience paying for the pop 
culture they love, which until a few years 
ago was subject to a clear-cut mix of fed- 
eral theft and copyright laws. Now these 
people are being 
called thieves 
and pirates. 

The Web has 
also highlighted 
the differences 
between intellectu- 
al and other 
kinds of prop- 
erty. “If you 
‘take’ my idea,” 
Lawrence Lessig 
writes in Code and 
Other Laws of 
Cyberspace, “I still 
have it. If I tell you an 

idea, you have not deprived 
me of it... Your consumption does not 
lessen mine. Ideas, at their core, can be 


shared with no reduction in the amount 
the ‘owner’ can consume. This difference 


is fundamental, and it has been under- 
stood since the founding.” 

In fact, Thomas Jefferson expressed one 
of his fondest wishes for intellectual prop- 
erty in an eerie fantasy that might have 
come directly out of the Internet: “That 
ideas should freely spread from one to 
another over the globe, for the moral and 
mutual instruction of man, and improve- 
ment of his condition, seems to have been 
peculiarly and benevolently designed by 
nature, when she made them, like fire, 
expansible overall space, without lessening 
their density at any point, and like the 
air...incapable of confinement or exclusive 
appropriation. Inventions then cannot, in 
nature, be a subject of property.” 


In the Digital 


Age, is it even {| ‘ 

possible to “own” As a writer, | long 
something dis- | 480 accepted 
tributed globally | that once ideas 
through a repre- | are released onto 
sentational medi- | the Net, they 

um like the Net? pass into a com- 


Asa writer, Ilong | | | 
agoacceptedthat \ pletely new realm 


once ideas are re- 

leased onto the Internet, they pass into a 
completely new realm (just ask Ken Starr). 
They are, as Jefferson suggested, no longer 
the individual property of their creator, 
like it or not. 

rhe structure of the Net—andthe Web | 
in particular—alters the way the next 
generation views many traditional ethics 
and values, including definitions of theft, 
content, and property. To see a couple of 
expressions of this sensibility, check out 
GEEK FORCE [www.geekforce.org] or 
NETROPOLIS [go.to/netropolis]. 

Under federal law, the government grants 


* 


copyright owners certain exclusive rights 
to their work, including the right to repro- 
duce it. But in the digital realm, there are 
countless partners, participants, and pub- 
lishers—tens of millions of people with 
computers and modems, along with the 
unpoliceable new means of transmission. 
Who owns ideas in the 21st century? For 
better or worse, we all do.

SEPARATING THE NEWS FROM THE NOISE 


Can ‘em 


THE PROMISE: Make cheap or free 
long-distance calls today over the Net! 
THE REALITY: Only if you are truly 
geeky or incredibly tightfisted. 


IDS MAKE “PHONES” BY CONNECTING 

two cans with a piece of string. Sim- 

ilarly, some grown-ups are now mak- 

ing long-distance “phone calls” by 

connecting two computers with a 
piece of Internet. 

Sadly, the quality is about the same. 
| Programs that allow you to make inex- 
pensive or free long-distance calls over 
the Internet, including NET2PHONE 
[net2phone.com], DIALPAD.cCoM, and 
DELTATHREE.COM, are gaining notoriety, 
primarily by 
promising to give 
you something 
fornothing. They 
use voice-over- 
Internet proto- 
col technology, 
which takes a 

voice call and splits 
it into “packets” of data that it then 
reassembles at the other end. 

Die-hard fans assert they can make free 
calls over their computers that are every 
bit as good as the pick-up-the-phone kind. 
Don't be fooled. Although Net calls have 
the potential for reducing or eliminating 
your long-distance phone bills some- 
day...that day is at least a year away. 

Take, for instance, my recent experi- 
ence with Net2phone. Far from enjoying 
a crisp, clear call, I thought I was trying 
to communicate through a solar storm 
| with someone ona distant planet. To add 
inconvenience to incomprehensibility, 
programs such as Dialpad allow you to 


You might as well 
string two cans 


together and yell 
like the devil 


Internet phone calls; 


make computer-to-computer calls only 
to others who have the same software. 

Probe Research expects Net-telephony call 
volume to jump to 4 billion minutes this 
year, from 2.5 billion in 1999. Market 
research firm Frost & Sullivan predicts that 
by 2006 it will grow to 634.5 billion minutes. 
As demand increases, the incentive to 
improve the transmission quality will most 
likely push Internet phone products to a 
moreacceptable level—at which point long- 
distance companies can start to worry. 

But until then, you might as well string 
two cans together and yell like the devil. 
Talk is cheap. (Good) long-distance phone 
calls still aren't. 


REALITY SCORE: 2 


HYPE REAL 


Getting lazy with 
Kozmo.com 


THE PROMISE: Instant gratification 
for the urban Webocracy. 

THE REALITY: As long as you don’t 
mind the markup. 

Why schlepp six blocks to the 7-Eleven, 
sixmore blocks to the video store, then six 
more blocks to the music store when you 
can walk six steps to your computer and 
get many of the same items delivered to 
your door in minutes? 

Why, indeed. New services such as 


URBAN FETCH [urbanfetch.com] and 
KOZMO.COM are turning the $60 billion 
convenience store industry on its head 
by offering free doorstep delivery of | 
impulse-buy items in under an hour. Just 
click, and they'll deliver videos, books, 
games, CDs, batteries, snack foods, some 
household items, cold and flu medicine— 
even condoms. 
Heck, for a small fee, they'll even come 
back and pick up your rented movie after 
you're done with it. 
The catch? So far, the services are avail- 
able in a limited number of U.S. cities, 
including New York, San Francisco, Seat- 
tle, Boston, and Washington. And all this 
indulgence comesata price: Kozmo marks 
up items as much as 40 percent, a lot like 
your local convenience store. 

Even so, it seems a small price to pay if 
you have...er...better things to do thanrun 
all over town ona hot summer night.

AOL and Yahoo!: Monopolies in the Making? 


In the wake of the Microsoft antitrust rulings, tech-industry watchers have speculated that other Internet giants might 
one day face government action. Bob Scheer and Jason McCabe Calacanis argue that AOL and Yahoo! could be next. 


They Should Go After AOL Next 
While the Justice Department now has 
hadits way with Microsoft, one has to won- 
der if government lawyers have ever heard 
of another rather large company called 
America Online. Surely the reason for 
breaking up huge companies ought to 
have something to do with the power those 
companies wield over our lives. Butif that’s 
the concern, AOL has amassed a power 
that Bill Gates can only dream about. 

Despite the unparalleled control over 
mass media in America represented by 
AOL's merger with Time Warner, there 
seems to be no concern at the Justice 
Department that AOL's bigness repre- 
sents a threat to the free market in that 
most important commodity of all—ideas. 
Even before its acquisition of America’s 

remier old media conglomerate—at 
$184 billion, the largest [such deal] ever— 
AOLrepresented the main portal to news, 
inancial services, and entertainment on 
the Internet for 22 million families. Not 
only does AOL guide those people to 
what's important on the Net, but the com 
pany obtains enormous personal data on 
its members. Perhaps for that reason, it 
as joined the financial services industry 
in fighting attempts to protect consumer 
privacy. 

What AOL represents is the classic 
threat of a huge conglomerate that wields 
its power and influence across the broad 
swath of the commercial and social 
aspects of our lives. AOL more than any 
company in America has the power to 
shape our consciousness. 

The government has focused on 
Microsoft, which has proved feeble at 
providing Web content and therefore 
represents little threat to free choice of 
information so vital to a democracy. But 
AOL has succeeded precisely because it 
is so effective in marketing content, 
including, most important, the news on 
whicha free citizenry relies. Even before 
its acquisition of the Time Warner media 
empire, AOL was the No. 1 source for 
news on the Web. CNN, which AOL 
picked up in the Time Warner deal, is the 
10th most important. 

Never in the history of news publishing 


has one company held such extensive 
power over what we see and hear as does 
AOLin the wake of the Time Warner deal. 
Nor is it a matter of merely dominating 
the all-important U.S. market. Obviously, 
Time Warner has a major impact on the 
world’s culture through movies and tele- 
vision in addition to print. And now, AOL 
is clearly contending for worldwide dom- 
inance. In a $8.2-billion deal to gain full 
control of AOL Europe and AOL Australia, 
AOL[earlier this year] bought out the 50% 
interest in those joint ventures of its part- 
ner, Germany's Bertelsmann group, the 
world’s biggest book publisher. 

If antitrust law does not speak to the 
threat that AOL represents over the entire 
range of our culture, then it is in desperate 
need of revision. Microsoft's control over 
its Windows operating system may have 
inconvenienced some software competi- 
tors, but AOL's acquisition of formerly com- 
peting media outlets is destructive of the 
free marketplace of ideas. 

Perhaps because of AOL’s emerging 
dominance, the news media, which have 
been scathing in their treatment of 
Microsoft, seem to have barely covered 
the potential threat of AOL. How long 
will the journalists and the government 
ignore the fact that in the new world of 
the information age, the specter of Big 
Brother is represented not by Microsoft 
but rather by AOL. —Bob Scheer 
[ojr.usc.edu/content/story.cfm?id=358]

Trust Busters Should Watch Yahoo! 
With all the media scrutiny and attention 
around the Microsoft antitrust case, I 
thought I would take a moment to share 
my theory that a) monopolies can actual- 
ly be good for consumers, albeit in the 
short term, and b) that the 800-pound 
gorilla everyone should really be focus- 
ing on is not AOL Time Warner or 
Microsoft, but Yahoo! 

[Yahoo!] is doing exactly what Microsoft 
didin the late 80s and into the 90s. Yahoo! 
is using its huge platform to incorporate 
other people’s clever ideas—and com- 
plete businesses, in some cases—into its 
product line. 

Anyone who used DOS and Windows in 
the early days remembers all the add-on 
utilities that have now become standard 
in the Windows operating system. There 
was a time when hard drive utilities such 
as notepads, calculators, and address books 
had to be purchased separately... 
Microsoft destroyed these companies by 
simply incorporating the functionality of 
their third-party software into the Win- 
dows operating system, giving the add-on 
product to consumers for free. Microsoft 
was occasionally sued for this, but often 
remained unchallenged. Until, of course, 
the trend became so apparent that the Jus- 
tice Department and local attorneys gen- 
eral had to become involved. 

At the end of the day users were, for the 
most part, better served getting these prod- 


fe 


WWW.YIL.COM 73 


ucts for free from Microsoft. However, the 
Microsoft versions of third-party utilities 
are typically not as feature-rich, and since 
Microsoft slowly destroyed all its com- 
petitors over time, users are now at the 
Goliath's mercy for product updates and 
new features. 

Microsoft is quite good at what it does, 
so aside from the fact that Windows 
crashed constantly for over a decade 
(nobody’s perfect!), there was never a major 
problem for consumers. The only truly 
problematic issue is the way Microsoft 
went about killing its competitors, which 
we all know was at best mercenary and at 
worst despicable (think Netscape). 

Yahoo! is doing the exact same thing 
today. Electronic party invitation sites such 
as Evite.com are just finding a foothold in 
the market—and now, Yahoo! has incor- 
porated their feature set. Want centralized 
bookmarks (Blink.com’s raison d’étre) and 
free hard drive space (as X:Drive offers)? 
Check out bookmarks.yahoo.com and 
briefcase.yahoo.com. Thanks to the Web’s 
open development standards, there are 
very few innovative applications that 
Yahool, or any other company witha solid 
development team, couldn't buildinacou- 
ple of days. 

The really powerful aspect of Yahoo!’s 
feature-rich system, however, is that 
when Yahoo! turns on a feature, tens of 
millions of registered users automatical- 
ly have the new functionality....Is there 
really a need fora free hard-drive provider 
or party invitation mailing service when 
Yahoo! incorporates those features into 
its own offerings?... 

The issue we need to examine is how 
Yahoo! is attacking its competitors. Is 
the company competing on merits alone 
or is it using its might to squash others in 
the field?... 

If you work for the Justice Department, 
I recommend slapping Microsoft with a 
nice large fine, using the money to put net- 
worked computers (sans Windows, of 
course) in every public school in the world, 
and then taking a very close look at 
Yahoo!'s tactics and business model. You 
might just find that the new boss is much 
the same as the old boss. 

It’sjust like in The Matrix—we are the 
batteries for Yahoo!’s engine. Take the 
red pill. —Jason McCabe Calacanis 
[siliconalleydaily.com] 


Southern Bull 


WAY SOUTH celebrates Southern culture. 
Here, site editor Grayson Hurst Daughters 
protests against what she considers unfair 
depictions of the South on the big screen. 


WHERE WOULD WE SOUTHERNERS BE 
without movies to perpetuate stereotypes 
we've never known ourselves? From Mid- 
night in the Garden of Good and Evil to Steel 
Magnolias, where else but from Hollywood 
can you still see women flouncing down 
the street in fussy dresses and straw hats, 
costumed like one of Flannery O’Conner’s 
[sic] pet chickens... 

And from the rural settings Hollywood 
isso taken with, you'd think all Southern- 
ers lived in asbestos shacks outfitted with 
screen doors one can conveniently slam to 
emphasize a liquored-up transgression. Of 
course, most Southerners live in stucco, 
asbestos-free cities like Houston, Atlanta, 
or Charlotte and shop at Pottery Barn for 
their porch accessories. 

Ignoring our many urban wonders, Hol- 
lywood consistently opts fora backwoods 
locale...to film against. What could possi- 
bly be so chronically attractive about 
cruddy little beauty parlors and chigger- 
ridden Spanish moss hanging from oak 
trees? Or twentysomething women out- 


fitted like Aunt Bea, as if not to frighten 
the locals?... 

How long can Hollywood continue to 
deny that Southerners now have running 
water, let alone satellite technology?... 

And haven't you grown tired of seeing 
Souther blacks only in roles that require 
them to dress in shabby outfits and play 
church for well-meaning white people, 
like they did in The Apostle?...Perhaps the 
greatest example of a Southern movie 
accent not found in nature is located in 
the opening scene of Prince of Tides. So ludi- 
crous is the drawl of the first bit of dialog, 
‘Ahhh gaaarooo-uuuup sloeahwoolee bah- 
sidah thaaaa tiidahs an’ thah mawhhshiz 
of tha’ Cay-row-linah sea eyeahlanz...’ that 
when we get to the part where the dis- 
gruntled spouse blurts out, ‘Ahh doan 
give’ah shit abou’thah Big Dippahhh!’ the 
characters have become emotionally con- 
fused FogHorn-LegHorns. By the time the 
movie has moved on to such classic lines 
as, ‘You're tough Lowenstein, and I’m hun- 
gry,’ Nick Nolte has given up all attempts 
at low-country dialect and just snorts and 
snaps like a New Jersey repairman... 
Luckily, we’re far too preoccupied with 
mixing bourbon highballs on the veranda 
to take much of this Hollywood nonsense 
very seriously. [waysouth.com/Culture/ 
filmed_south.html] 


BARRIE ROKEACH

So modern it has a setting for 
video games and digital media, 
so retro it sports an analog clock, 
Philips’s 13-inch NUVISION TV 
() looksas ifit were lifted from 
the Jetsons’ Skypad apartment.

tastes? Optimize the sound and 
picture for sports, movies, 
music, and other programming 
at the push of a button. And if 
you tend to nod off in front of 
the nightly news, the NuVi- 
sion’s feet double as night- 
lights....Here in the States, radio 
is one of the last bastions of 
analog sound, and—despite 
the claims of Steely Dan—even 
FM is susceptible to static.

the BBC offers digital broad- 
casts in tandem with its analog 
fare and commissioned Ideo 
Design to dream up a concept 
for a DIGITAL RADIO (2). In 
addition to delivering crystal- 
clear sound, digital signals can 
carry extra information, so the 
tadio would have a screen to

Angels in cyberspace: 
suiardians of the Net 


An army of virtual volunteers patrols the 


_ Internet to protect you and your children 


WO YEARS AGO, PARRY AFTAB SAW SOMETHING ON HER COMPUTER 


screen that changed her life: a graphic depiction of a toddler being 


subjected to horrifying sexual abuse. “I thought I was an expert on 


pedophilia, but I’d never seen it up close,” Aftab says. Unable to 
rid her mind of the image, Aftab resolved to fight the trafficking 


of such pictures on the Internet and to | 
dedicate her life to protecting children 
from sexual predators. | 
As executive director of CYBERANGELS. | 
ore, Aftab now oversees an army of 4,000 
volunteers who patrol the Net for 
pedophilia and educate kids about Inter- 
net safety. But children aren’t the only | 
ones who benefit: The group also 
responds to emergency calls from adult 
victims of cyberstalkers. Every day, near- 
ly 500 desperate Net users contact 
Cyberangels after being harassed. Some of 
the threats turn out to be amateurish 
teenage pranks, but others are orches- 
trated with disturbing precision. 
Asurfernamed Mary was chatting online 
when she got involved in what she consid- 
ered harmless banter. “I’m smarter than you 
are,” someone wrote. Mary replied: “You 
thinkso, do ya?” Piqued by herresponse, the 
chat partner proceeded to shut down Mary’s 
computer, using hacking software to take 
control of her system remotely. When the 
cyberstalker continued to torment herand 
disrupt her computer use, Mary sought help 
and found Cyberangels. Volunteers used the 
stalker’s ICQ screen name to track down | 
his IP address, his ISP, and his physical loca- | 
| 


tion. Confronted with this information and 
Cyberangels’ demands that he cease his 
harassment or face a police investigation, 
the stalker retreated. The case was closed. 

Others are not so fortunate. “In some 
cases, there are serious death threats,” Aftab 
explains. “People write, I know yourlicense 
plate number, where you live, and where 
your children go to school.’” Stalkers use 
simple Web searches, online directories, 
and software suchas Back Orifice to find out 
as much as they can about a potential tar- 
get. (Often sent as a disguised attachment, | 
Back Orifice is a program that allows hack- 
ers to get into a computer and operate it 
remotely.) They then use the information 
to harass, blackmail, and even confront the 
victim in person. In situations that escalate 
in this way, Cyberangels gathers info on the 
suspect and works with the police to track 
that person down. 

Before Aftab took the helm in 1998, 
Cyberangels had meager financial support 
and a three-year history of barely manag- 
ing to make ends meet. At first, she strug- 
gled to keep the site afloat, selling her 
house and using much of her retirement

COMPARING THE TRIED-AND-TRUE WITH THE NET-AND-NEW By DAVID SHEFF 


Buying a dishwasher 


OLD WAY 


Last time, the dishwasher needed to be 

repaired; this time, it died. I called two dish- 
washer repair outfits and asked for recommen- 
dations about the most trouble-free brands. 
The consensus? The basic KitchenAid model. 


Called local appliance stores—both inde- 
pendents and chains. The best price was 
$547, plus $95 for delivery and installation. 


TIME ELAPSED: 


40 minutes 


Z COMMENTS: in my experience, repair 


people who have no alliance with any 
brand give the most reliable advice. 


NET WAY 


Surfed to CONSUMER REPORTS ONLINE 

[consumerreports.org] and registered. (The 
service costs a modest $24 a year; you can 
also choose to pay $4 a month.) The site’s top 
recommendation was a Maytag model, so I 
decided that’s what I wanted. 


I went to GOTO.coM to compare online 
prices. Other major brands were included, 


| but not Maytag. 


I found a site called BEST MAYTAG HOME 

APPLIANCE CENTER [bestmaytag.com], 
but the company delivers only in the Los 
Angeles area, where it is based. 


At MAYTAG.CoM, I found a toll-free number. 

After checking with his supervisor, the 
representative explained that there is no offi- 
cial online merchant for Maytag products. 


| He suggested a discount store near my home. 


I called and was given the price: $560, plus 
$100 for delivery and installation. Placed an 
order over the phone. 


TIME ELAPSED: 


26 minutes 


COMMENTS: The Net Way could have won if | 
had selected one of the other popular brands. 


> WINNER: TIE Net Way to choose the dishwasher brand and model, Old Way to buy it.

NEWS MAKERS IN THE ONLINE COMMUNITY sy BILGE EBIRI 


Action Jackson 


Congressman -webmaster Jesse Jackson Jr. 
on the quest lor a more perfect wired union 


ESSE JACKSON JR. WAS ALMOST NAMED SELMA. HIS FATHER, THE REV- 
erend Jesse Jackson, was marching from Selma, Alabama, to Mont- 
gomery when he was born, at the height of the civil rights 
struggle. In later years, Jesse Jr. accompanied his father on 
campaign tours and on attempts to secure the release of hostages 


in places like Syria. Thus, few were sur- 
prised in 1995 when the younger Jackson 
himself entered politics, representing the 
Second Congressional District of Illinois. 

Today, with a political persona that 
embraces both grand causes and practical 
initiatives, Jackson is one of the rising stars 
of the Democratic Party. Many see him run- 
ning for the Senate in 2004; there have even 
been whispers of the presidency. In fact, in 
a symbolic passing of the torch, Jesse Sr. 
announced his decision not to run for office 
in 2000 through his son’s official Web site, 
JESSE JACKSON, JR. [jessejacksonjr.org]. 

Andit’s the Net that distinguishes Jack- 
son most among his peers. He is the force 
behind Congress's study of online voting, 
and his use of the Internet has become leg- 
endary. During Senate hearings on Carol 
Moseley-Braun’s nomination as ambas- 
sador to New Zealand and Samoa, Jackson's 
e-mail alerts prompted thousands of 
calls—effectively shutting down phone 
lines—to the office of Sen. Jesse Helms, the 
chairman of the Foreign Affairs Commit- 
tee, who staunchly opposed the nomina- 
tion. Among the most wired of politicians, 
Jackson is paged automatically every time 
someone gives money to his campaign, 
and he calls each donor back, often with- 
in minutes. 
Jackson’s key concern about the Netis the 
digital divide, which prevents lower- 
income Americans from taking advantage 
of the Web's opportunities and which he 
believes to be only one aspect of a larger 
social and infrastructural schism. 

In person, Jackson displays all the elo- 
quence and rhetorical flourishes of his 
father. Equally comfortable talking about 
Pentium processors and issues ofrace and 
economic opportunity, he’s bursting with 
ideas: At one point, he thinks up a pro- 
posal requiring planes in flight to upload 
their data to secure Web sites—an alter- 
native to “black boxes"—and leaps to his 
feet, instructing his legislative director to 
take notes. Indeed, for Jesse Jackson Jr., 
passion is something that can encompass 
both processors and politics. 


Y-LIFE: How did you first discover the 
Internet? 
JACKSON: | used to be the national field 
director of the Rainbow Coalition. One day 
we found out that President Clinton was 


PAUL ELLEDGE 


90 JULY 2000 YAHOO! INTERNET LIFE 


going to withdraw the nomination of Lani 
Guinier for assistant Attorney General. My 
father got on a plane and must have gone 
to 10 different cities trying to tell people 
we'd better do something. He ended up at 
Howard University, very sick from exhaus- 
tion.SoI created the Jax Fax, a one-page fax 
on some critical issue, which we sent to 
thousands of people every week. When I 
first sent it, in June of 1993, it must have 
taken 12 hours to send it to all the people 
on the list. Then it began to grow. And this 
became our equivalent of getting the Rev- 

_ erend Jackson on a plane. Bill Cosby, 
Richard Pryor, Arsenio, and Stevie Wonder 
were all receiving the Jax Fax. 

At a point when there were probably 
about five pages on the Internet, I tried to 
tell my father that we had a very under- 
funded field department and that there was 
anew medium that was emerging, At that 
time his mind was so obsessed with fax- 
ing, because a lot of people were getting it, 
and not everybody was so savvy. But it 
opened my mind to the possibilities of this 
new technology. 

Y-LIFE: How would you describe the 
digital divide? 

JACKSON: The divide has to be looked 
at in two categories. One is the infra- 
structural divide, which has everything to 
do with wiring our schools, everything 
to do with the affordability of a laptop, 
everything to do with whether teachers 
can integrate that technology with the 
basic curriculum for students. And then 
there’s the huge education divide. I 
recently did a television show that airs 
primarily in Silicon Valley, and one young 
African-American was approached dur- 
ing the interview and asked if he wanted 
a computer. He said, “What do I need a 
computer for? What’s that going to do for 
me?” That's part of the education divide. 

Y-LIFE: Has the government been effec- 
tive in helping to bridge the divide? 

JACKSON: The current administration 
has helped what it understands to be the 
possibilities of the Web. But even here on 
Capitol Hill, there's still a great divide. Most 
members of Congress still relegate the 
Internet to the lowest-paid person on their 
staff. In my office, I'm the webmaster of 
[my site]. So many proposals and recom- 
mendations aren't coming from people 
who have the best information about the 


Net and the use of the medium itself. And 
that’s at every level of the government. In 
other words, the digital divide is not just 
occurring in the broader American public; 
it’s also occurring among our leadership. 

Y-LIFE: What about the private sector? 
Microsoft, for example, has a relationship 
now with Black Entertainment Television 
[BET], and AOL and NetNoir have an- 
nounceda partnership. To what extentcan 
they help? 

JACKSON: The problem with these ven- 
turesis that [although] they promote multi- 
culturalism on the Web, they don'taddress 


‘Here’s a great idea: 
Why not deliver 
groceries online? 
Now, what happens 
when your grocery 
deliveries don’t 
cross 55th Street 
in Chicago?” 


the infrastructural issues that I’ve raised. It 
is not the private sector's responsibility, 
norisit the aim of the private sector, to pro- 
vide everyone in America with a laptop. 
One of our presidents ran on the theme 
of putting a chicken in every pot. Who is 
the presidential candidate who is running 
to provide a laptop in every schoolbag? 
Whois the candidate running to putacom- 
puterin every home? Whois the candidate 
to provide tax incentives for senior citizens 
and working-class Americans to go back to 
school and take some basic computer lit- 
eracy course so that they might become 
functional in thenew medium? You've not 
heard any of these ideas because they're 
not happening in the public sector. 
Y-LIFE: But they are happening in the 
private sector, with companies like Ford 
giving computers to all their employees. 
JACKSON: But that was a labor agree- 
ment. And while the private sector can 
achieve a number of philanthropic goals 
in this area, its primary responsibility is to 
maximize profit. They might set up foun- 
dations that give computers to some 


schools that have been left behind, and 
some might work with BET to covera cul- 
tural diversity flank. But the goal of putting 
acomputerin every home cannot possibly 
be led by the private sector. 

Y-LIFE: I think the idea of a laptop in 
every home was one of Newt Gingrich’s 
campaign proposals some years ago. Do 
you think he was genuine in this desire? 

JACKSON: I don’t know what Speaker 
Gingrich’s motives were. In politics, motives 
are often secondary to effects. What is the 
result ofa proposal with respect to my con- 
stituents[and] the American people? That's 
the most important question for me. The 
Speaker's goal was legitimate, whatever his 
motives. Computers and computer profi- 
ciency are the future. If you don’t know, 
don’t understand, or are afraid of comput- 
ers, your future is probably not bright. 

Y-LIFE: Do you find that either party 
tends to cater more to the technologically 
oriented than the other? 

JACKSON: don'tsee either party orany 
of the candidates willing to put their trea- 
sure—the federal budget—where their 
mouth is with respect to putting a laptop 
in every house. And that includes Newt. In 
the name of “balancing the budget,” 
“reducing the debt,” and being “fiscally 
responsible”—all right and proper posi- 
tions, in proportion to the nation's GNP— 
too many of today’s politicians aren't will- 
ing to invest in the infrastructure to make 
and keep America the strongest nation in 
the world for tomorrow and the next day. 
If we can’t afford to do that today, with a 
$9 trillion economy that dwarfs every 
other economy in the world, and with siz- 
able budget surpluses, when can we? 

Y-LIFE: There are those who say that the 
divide is an issue not of haves and have- 
nots, but of haves and have-laters—that 
the white middle and upper classes have 
always been at the forefront of technolog- 
ical change, and others will follow. 

JACKSON: I have a Pentium II comput- 
er. We just bought some Pentium IIIs, and 
they haven't come out of the box yet. There 
aresome in my district that don’teven have 
286 computers. By the time some of our 
students are accessing 386s and 486s and 
Pentium I machines, the rest of the society 
has moved on to a new level of economic 
growth and development. So those who 
make that argument have fallen into the 


www.vtt.com 9f 


Y-LIFE INTERVIEW 


classic trap—that somehow the private sec- 
torand the private market will lift the boats 
that are stuck at the bottom. 

Look at it this way: When Clinton 
became president there were about 33 mil- 
lion Americans who didn’t have health 
care. Today there are about 45 million. As 
robust as the economy has been, the pri- 
vate sector has not been able to provide 
everyone with health care. The housing 
crisis is getting worse. Somehow, there’s 
this theory, this thinking, that unabetted 
and unaided, the private sector is going to 
help those who have been left behind. And 
the reality is that there’s no history of that 
in this country. 

Y-LIFE: What about television? Origi 
nally, it was suburban, white households 
that got it. Now everybody does. 

JACKSON: But not everybody has a 
remote control. And not everybody has 
cable. Not everybody has WebTV. Not 
everybody has a TV set that’s connected to 
the Internet. So there are significant gra- 
dations of the problem. In Appalachia, 
where there are mostly poor whites, there’s 
no cable yet. They still have rabbit-ear 
antennas for their television sets. 

Y-LIFE: Will the Net make a difference 
in the election? 

JACKSON: | proposed a piece of legisla- 
tion calling fora study of the Web asa vehi 
cle for casting one’s ballot, as a means of 
expanding democracy. Right now, ona cold 
day voters in Chicago have to get up in the 
morning and, on their way to work, go to 
the polling place and cast a ballot for me 
or do it at the conclusion of the working 


DOT-GOV: Whether in Chicago or Washington, Jackson stays connected to his constituents. 


day, go and cast a vote on my behalf. If they 
are late for work or might miss work, 
they're not likely to vote. If they get caught 
in traffic, they're not likely to vote. 
Y-LIFE: How would online voting work? 
JACKSON: My Internet voting study 
would theoretically allow [voters] the 
opportunity to cast their ballot from a 
secure Web site on behalf of their con- 
gressman. Polling places will become 
portable. Senior citizens will no longer 
have to be bused to a polling place; some- 
one can show up with a laptop at their 
senior citizens’ home, or there can be a 


basic PC at the home that allows them to 
do it securely. Same goes for hospitals or 
for people in neighborhoods that are dif- 
ficult to access. Any PC can be a portable 
polling place. So it has the opportunity 
and potential to multiply the number of 
polling places for participation. Obvious- 
ly, there are security issues that members 
of Congress, both Republican and Demo- 
crat, have raised, which only a study can 
begin to address. 

My goalis not to be technologically futur- 
istic but [rather] to get more people voting. 
We need more democracy, not less. Every 


PAUL ELLEDGE 


American should vote and be able to vote. 
My goal is to make Internet voting efficient, 
effective, and secure. Exactly how it will 
work is what my legislation is about: to 
study the possibility of Internet voting— 
its potential benefits and potential prob- 
lems. My legislation does not bring about 
Internet voting. It is a study to clear the 
way for the possibility of Internet voting. 

Y-LIFE: Do you eventually see a virtual 
Congress? 

JACKSON: | support a virtual Congress. 
I should be able to cast a vote on the floor 
of the Congress from anywhere in the coun- 
try. But those of us who support a virtual 
Congress are few and far between. When I 
go to vote I have to carry this card that I stick 
in the voting machine on the floor of the 
Congress. I could [theoretically] use that 
from anywhere. 

The problem is that then no member of 
Congress would come to Washington. 
There is this genuine desire to have mem- 
bers present, so that you don’t have these 
big empty chambers and everybody else is 
sitting on a beach somewhere in the 
Bahamas drinking a pina colada, while 
their staffis casting their vote on some crit- 
ical issue facing the nation. 

Y-LIFE: How has Congress adapted to 
the Web? 

JACKSON: Most members of Congress 
havea Website that’s paid for by the Mem- 
bers’ Representational Allowance [MRA]. I 
don't. Everything that congressmen do, 
everything that they send out of their office, 
is subject to a whole bunch of House rules 
that are too numerous to go through. And 


that’s why when you get a letter from a 
congressman, it hardly says anything. 
Those same rules apply to how we spend 
the $875,000 we get every year torun these 
offices, which means that the money we 
spend on the Web site must comply with 
those rules. 

Ihave my own campaign site. Everything 
I do in Congress I can post on this site, 
because my campaign has the right to do 
research on what I'm doing in Congress. 
But on my campaign site I get to ask for 
money. | get to say why I support [a partic- 
ular] bill. I’ve collected about 8,000 e-mail 
addresses since I've been online. I can take 
an e-mail message and send it to 8,000 peo- 
ple,andsome subset of 8,000 will call acon- 
gressman and say, “We want you to sign on 
to this bill.” As a matter of fact, I shut Jesse 
Helms's phones down for two weeks. 

Y-LIFE: During the Carol Moseley-Braun 
confirmation hearings, right? 

JACKSON: Correct. And then his office 
was combing through my Web site trying 
to find out if there was some violation in 
the use of my site. None. Because my cam- 
paign Web site is not a House Web site. 
Most members of Congress who collect 
e-mail addresses cannot send an e-mail 
challenging another member of Congress, 
because they collected those e-mail ad- 
dresses using public funds, so their sites 
are frozen. The limitation ofall MRA funds 
is that members cannot do anything polit- 
ical on their government Web site. 

For example, by using my campaign site, 
it took me only three months to secure 74 
cosponsors for my HOPE for Africa legis- 


lation, H.R. 772, while the sponsor of the 
African Growth and Opportunity Act took 
over three years to enlist 75 cosponsors. I 
could communicate quickly with people 
and groups all across the country who 
could then contact their congressperson 
and urge them to support my legislation. 
I had colleagues in Congress come up to 
me and say that it was the most effective 
lobbying campaign that they had ever 
seen. 

Y-LIFE: Aren't some of your colleagues 
resentful, though? They have to play by 
certain rules, and you're playing by dif- 
ferent ones. 

JACKSON: No, on the contrary. It’s not a 
function of whether I’m playing by a dif- 
ferent set of rules or whether I'm not fol- 
lowing any law. I’m following the letter of 
the law. But at the same time, I’m under no 
obligation to have a congressional Website. 
I choose to have a Web site that’s totally 
unrelated to the Second Congressional Dis- 
trict of Illinois. It’s the Jesse Jackson Jr. for 
Congress Committee [Web site], which can 
do everything that the other does, but with 
more freedom. Why should! put up a Web 
site that can’t say anything and only hasmy 
bio? It shows just how antiquated some of 
the rules are, here in the House, in light of 
the new technology. 

Y-LIFE: What do you think is the most 
important piece of Net-related legislation 
coming up before Congress? 

JACKSON: In the last Congress, the most 
important piece of Internet-related legisla- 
tion was the e-rate, where we mandated that 
those who received access to the public air-

I think that, going into the 
future, maybe the most signif. 
icant piece of legislation will 
be central to the question of 
the divide. It appears to me that 
the country is going to needa 
significant amount of revenue 
in order for everyone to take 
advantage of the Internet. If 
not, for example, the Internet 
itselfis only going to bea reflec- 
tion of the broader society. 

Y-LIFE: What do you mean 
by that? How willit reflect the 
broader society? 

JACKSON: Here’s a great 
idea: Why not deliver groceries 
online? So youstarta big ware- 
house, with a bunch of fresh 
fruit in it, and you start deliv- 
ering groceries. Now, what hap- 
pens when your grocery deliv- 
eries don’t cross 55th Street in 
Chicago, because you prefer to 
deliver to brownstones? Well, 
now you've done what they 
call redlining. So now we have 
to extend antidiscriminatory 
redlining legislation to deliv- 
ery companies that determine 
which neighborhoods they 
will deliver to,and which ones 
they won't. 

So to that extent, the Net 
becomes only a reflection of 
problems that exist in the 
broader society. We are as 
diverse as possible in sharing 
ideas on the Internet, but the 
reality is that in the delivery of 
services, we have the same 
problems that exist in the 
broader society. So you can 
count ona number of pieces of 
egislation as many of these 
companies end up before the 
ustice Department and in fed: 
eral court on why they’re not 
delivering in certain areas. 
That's going to be asignificant 
problem. 

Y-LIFE: Recently, there has 
been some controversy about 
Kozmo.com, the online urban 
delivery site, allegedly not 


delivering to black neighbor- 
hoods. Do you see this occur- 
ring more and more? 

JACKSON: I have not yet 
heard of other instances of 
{alleged] redlining, other than 
Kozmo, but that doesn’t mean 
they don’t exist. In fact, even 
without evidence, I strongly 
suspect there are many more. 
The combination of class and 
caste are part of the American 
experience. With class, busi- 
nesspeople target the top of 
the income scale—where the 
money is. With caste, institu- 
tional racism, not always per- 
sonal racism, makes the 
assumption that blacks don’t 
have money to spend on the 
nternet, or they just ignore 
blacks with money. That 
seems irrational and uneco- 
nomic, but racism is irrational 
and uneconomic. 

Y-LIFE: Should e-commerce 
be taxed? 

JACKSON: | don't know ifit 
should be done, but it should 
be considered. think that you 
can't build, on the one hand, a 
more perfect union for every 
American and then turn 
around and say that we have 
to limit all avenues for raising 
revenue in the future that 
might provide the very secu- 
rity we need in order to con- 
tinue to do business. 

The Net was never designed 
to be a vehicle to be unpatri- 
otic. Before, it was unavailable 
to the public—it was a gov- 
ernment-run institution in 
the Defense Department. And 
then it became declassified, 
and much more usable for 
more people. Well, at the 
same time, the country has a 
crumbling infrastructure. So 
if all businesses in America 
decide to go online and aban- 
don their shop fronts, then 
the government could be out 
of business. I think even the 
most liberal or most conserv- 
ative among us would agree 
that that’s unreasonable.

ARNING: 


STUDIES SAY NET USE CAN CAUSE 
DEPRESSION, ISOLATION, ADDICTION. 
Y-LIFE PULLS BACK THE CURTAIN ON 
SOME SHAKY SCIENCE 


“MY MOM E-MAILED ME TODAY,” SAYS 


Dave Kansas, the 33-year-old editor in chief of 
TheStreet.com. “And she says, ‘I just read a survey 
about people who are on the Internet a lot, and it 
says that they start to withdraw from society. 
Some even forget to call their mother.’” He stops. 
“I wrote back, and I said, ‘Who are you again?’” 

Well, Dave, join the (lonely) crowd. Ofall the 
Internet stories to captivate America—from 
Napster to the couple promising to lose their 
virginity in front of a Web cam—none has 
proved better catnip for the media than the 
recent finding that one in seven online users is 
in danger of losing contact with “real human 
beings.” That statistic was the result ofa survey 
conducted by the Stanford Institute for the 
Quantitative Study of Society. The more people 
used the Internet, the study concluded, the 
more anemic their offline social life became. 
Suddenly, surfing the Internet seemed to have 
a karmic comeuppance: e-strangement. 

The day after the study was released earlier this 
year, the results were splashed on the front pages 
of two of the country’s most respected newspa- 
pers. “A Newer, Lonelier Crowd Emerges in Inter- 
net Study,” announced The New York Times. Under 
the headline “A Web of Workaholic Misfits? 


www.yit.com {Of 


INTERNETMADNESS! 


Study Finds Heavy Internet Users Are 
Socially Isolated,” The Washington Post 
intoned that “the Internetis creating a class 
of people who...are so solitary they can hard- 
ly be bothered to call Mom on her birth- 
day.” (Note to Mrs. Kansas: Don’t blame 
Dave.) Riding the tidal wave of coverage, 
the study's principal investigator, Norman 
Nie, made the rounds on TY, “I knew this 
was an important study,” Nie says. “But I 
had no idea it would be so explosive.” 

Nie’s report wasn't the first Internet study 
to make the front page. Appearing earlier 
was HomeNet, which was unveiled in the 
fall of 1998. In that study, a team of re- 
searchers at Carnegie Mellon examined Net 
users in Pittsburgh over two years. Their 
alarming finding? Getting online entailed 
the distinct possibility of getting depressed. 

Two years before that, Kimberly Young, 
an assistant professor of psychology at the 
University of Pittsburgh, adopted the term 
Internet addiction disorder (the punch line 
ofajoke propagated on the Web) and made 
a career out of it—her reputation based on 
a pathology no one has proved actually 
exists. Based on her study of 396 regular 
Internet users and a control group of 100, 
and using the criteria for gambling addic- 
tion, Young concluded that there is such a 
thing as Internet addiction. 

If you take these three studies at their 
word, the picture isn’t pretty. Imagine 100 
serious online users at a swanky dot-com 
launch party: Of those, 25 would be turn- 
ingintoshut-ins, eight would be trolling for 
porn about 11 hours per week, and six 
would be flat-out addicted. And they’re all 
getting about 1 percentmore bummed just 
from logging on. Nothing—except maybe 
hard drugs or nuclear radiation—produces 
these kinds of negative side effects, The 
Net, the studies want you to believe, is caus- 
ing an epidemic of bad mojo. 

But take a closer look at the studies, and 
their design, methodology, and conclusions 
start to look suspect. “These studies all suf- 
fer from the same problem—some of these 
researchers seem to have a problem under- 
standing the basic research tools of social 
science,” says John Grohol, the cofounder 
of MHN: MENTAL HEALTH NET and the author 
of The Insider’s Guide to Mental Health 
Resources Online. The cumulative findings 
of the research—that the Internet causes 
depression, isolation, and addiction— 
might make for a great media virus and 
alarming headlines, but they are overgen- 
eralized, argues Howard Rheingold, author 


of The Virtual Community. Rheingold likens 
the studies to “analyzing the feeding pat- 
terns of fish by sitting on a mountain and 
looking at the ocean through binoculars.” 

Which certainly doesn’t make for credi- 
ble sociology. Charles Brownstein, an exec- 
utive director at the Corporation for 
National Research Initiatives, a private »on- 
profit that supports research in the public 


NOTHING—EXCEPT MAYBE 
HARD DRUGS OR NUCLEAR 
RADIATION—PRODUCES SUCH 
NEGATIVE SIDE EFFECTS AS 
CYBERSPACE. THE 
NET, THE STUDIES WANT YOU 
TO BELIEVE, IS CAUSING AN 
EPIDEMIC OF BAD MOJO 


interest, has serious doubts about the valid- 
ity of the three studies under discussion. 
“I've been doing social science for over 20 
years,” says Brownstein, who as a deputy 
assistant director of the National Science 
Foundation led research studies on the 
Internet. “And I’m astounded at the low 
quality of what we're seeing.” 

The first problem with the Stanford and 
HomeNet studies, notes Grohol, is that they 
may have prejudiced their pool of subjects. 
The Stanford team gave away 35,000 WebTV 
sets with free Internet access to households 
to facilitate the research; HomeNet distrib- 
uted Macs to its 169 subjects, who didn’t 
have computers and had never used the 
Internet. WebTV is an odd way to measure 
Net usage, given that fewer than 5 percent 
of surfers use it to get online. What's more, 
all Internet access is not equal. (Consider 
the apricotiMacon the T-1 at your office ver- 
sus the 28.8 WebTV unit Grandma has and 
you'll see the point.) And just 10 percent of 
all Internet users have free access, so neither 
study is particularly representative. 

There's one more problem in the mix: A 
person's relationship to a device that he 


pays for is different from his relationship 
to one that he gets for free, says Grohol. 
“There may be expectancies in the subjects, 
like, ‘I'd better use this computer because 
that’s what they want me to do.’ But the 
researchers didn’t measure for that.” 

The missing measurements almost out- 
number those used. The Stanford study 
asked 21 questions, and only two touched 
specifically on isolation. The results: Of 
“serious users” (according to the study, those 
who clock more than 10 hours a week 
online), 27 percent spend less time talking 
to friends and family on the phone, 15 per- 
cent spend less time with family and friends 
in person, and 13 percent go to fewer events 
outside the home. According to Nie, the 
team was “blindsided” by that erosion of 
social contact and would have asked more 
about it had they anticipated it. 

It’'snotsimply whatyouask, though, but 
whom and when. At the start, the Home- 
Net researchers probed their subjects’ state 
of mind and well-being by getting their 
reaction to statements like “I can findcom- 
panionship when I want it.” Though this 
did provide a preliminary basis for the 
study's findings, the researchers should 
have probed even further, reaching back 
into each person's history to establish what 
was inherent in a subject’s personality, 
what could be attributed to mood, and 
what was caused by the Internet. 

The HomeNet researchers tested the 
responses of their subjects twice: after one 
year and again a year later, with question- 
naires and home visits. The Stanford 
researchers compiled their findings based 
on responses taken only once so far (fol- 
low-ups are planned). Young’s Internet 
addiction surveys were conducted via polls 
at MSNBC.com and ABCNews.com. With- 
outa control group or psychological com- 
posites of subjects to provide a context for 
the results, the takers of the surveys have 
to hope that subjects weren't just cranky 
the day the questions were asked. Ever 
havea day when you turn the phone ringer 
off or ignore your cell phone? Our desire 
to communicate—which affects our atti- 
tudes about communication technolo- 
gies—follows our mood. 

Better surveys would have more rigor- 
ously tracked their subjects’ state of mind. 
HomeNet lead researcher Robert Kraut 
acknowledges that the study has limitations 
because of its sample group; for example, it 
included few people between the ages of 22 
and 40 (a key user group) and used only Pitts- 


FOR SITES IN THIS STORY, PLEASE TURN TO 


THIS MONTH'S SITE ADDRESS GUIDE, OR VISIT WWW.YIL.COM/URL/0007/MADNESS.HTML. 


102 suLy 2000 


burgh residents. “We hada trade-off,” Kraut 
explains. “We did an in-depth lookata local 
sample rather than a superficial look at a 
broadly representative sample.” New 
research from the HomeNet team is using 
national surveys. But Kraut stands by the 
original HomeNet study and argues that 
otherresearchers and journal editors want- 
edto poke holesin the work simply because 


it contradicted prevailing wisdom—that | 


the Net is an undisputed good thing. “I’m 
disappointed with some of the social sci- 
entists’ critiques, the ones that don't like 
the answer,” he says, “There is some classic 
research on journal editing which says that 
when research finds results inconsistent | 
with the current paradigm, reviewers tend | 
to find more flaws in the methodology. In 
part, I think that’s what we're seeing with | 
the critique of HomeNet.” 

Even with perfectly devised questions, | 


identifying the Net as the cause of isola- | 


tion and depression remains a tricky 
endeavor. The HomeNet subjects didn't | 
have computers or Net connections before 
the experiment began; it’s possible they | 
had no compelling desire to get online in 
the first place. “Maybe it’s depressing to go 


heingoldin the new foreword to The Vir- 
tual Community. Or perhaps lonely people 
are drawn online, struggling to find the 
community they miss in their physical 
ives. Either way, we don’t know, and 
assigning a cause and effect is dubious. 

“Communication is an ecological 
process, and youcan't pullone thing out | 
and examine it,” says Paul Levinson, a 
communications professor at Fordham 
niversity and author of The Soft Edge: A 
Natural History and Future of the Informa- 
tion Revolution. Levinson points to parallels 
with television. In one now-infamous 
study, parents were asked to keep track of 
what shows their kids watched; then the 
shows were rated for violence. The parents 
were alsoasked how many times their kids 
behaved violently, and “usually there was | 


a high correlation between what they | 


online if you don't really want to,” writes |) 


watched and what they did,” saysLevinson. | 


“The conclusion peoplecometoisthatTV | 


causes the violence. That A causes B. But | 
then again, violent kids might be drawn to 
violent TV.SoB might cause A.” OrC might 
trigger E, which encourages A, only when 
Dis in the room. In other words, correla- 
tion and causation are two different things. 


Easily explained “conclusions” also | 


crop up in the surveys. One of the major | 


findings of the Stanford study was 152 >> | 


BY LISA KO 


Will the Net destroy civilization? It won't be the first innovation to ruin us all. Alarmists 
have been declaring impending doom ever since some ingenious Stone Ager discov- 
ered fire. Here are the top [0 technological evils that once struck fear in skeptical hearts. 


| THE WHEEL (3500 B.C.) 
The arrival of the round wonder 
allowed prehistoric families to 
divvy up tasks, instead of hunt- 
ing and gathering as a team. 
Suicide rates skyrocketed as 
division of labor took our 
species into the Isolation Age. 


? THE TELEGRAPH (A.D. 
1837) Carpal tunnel syndrome’s 
granddaddy, telegrapher’s paral- 
ysis, was the hot workplace 
injury over a century ago. While 
hard-tapping Morse coders 
worked their fingers to the bone, 
others decried electricity as the 
devil's spawn. Meanwhile, Pony 
Express drivers hit the unem- 
ployment lines. 


3 THE TYPEWRITER (1867) 
Typewritten correspondence is 
alienating and soulless, cried 
19th-century critics. Much later, 
Anatole Broyard, editor at The 
New York Times Book Review, 
attributed the decline of letters 
to the loss of longhand: “What 
can you express, after all, ona 
typewriter?” 


4 RADIO (1895) Orson Welles 
broadcast his War of the 
Worlds prank on the airwaves 
long before the Good Times 
virus stirred up hysteria online. 


5 THE AUTOMOBILE (1896) 
The dawning of the age of the 
car let family members skip town 
on a moment's notice. Employ- 
ees drove Model T’s to the office 
instead of taking the trolley. The 
result? Longer working hours 


and higher divorce rates, not to 
mention more highway accidents. 


G TELEVISION (1924) The 
electronic baby-sitter softened 
brains and made us socially inept. 
But after decades of antitube 
rhetoric, studies now say we're 
lonely because we surf the Net 
too much and watch less TV. 


7 WIRELESS TELEPHONE 
(1946) In 1993, David Reynard 
claimed his late wife's cell phone 
had caused her brain tumor. 
Today, faithful readers of techno- 
phobe Ann Landers lobby for cell 
phone regulation. 


8 CABLE TV (1948) Fox exec 
Sy Salkowitz once likened the 
power of cable to “a cancer” 
“The networks will grow so rapid- 
ly that no one could possibly 
absorb all the information,” he 
asserted, “How can we maintain 


mastery and not become slaves?” 
Wonder how Salkowitz feels 
about the Game Show Channel. 


9 THE VCR (1969) In 1978, 
Newsweek predicted the era of 
“Viewer Lib,” when VCRs would 
tear apart the American family: no 
more family dinners, no more 
heart-to-hearts. Everyone's alone, 
convening with video. When was 
this slated to happen? 1985. 


{0 THE ATM (1969) A decade 
ago, The Plain Dealer proclaimed 
that ATMs would isolate bank 
employees and malfunction: “Like 
so many dreams, [it] may not 
come true.” Who's dreaming now? 


www.yil.com 103 


Inside 
a Silicon Alley studio, 
a wild bunch is scrambling to 
create live video shows for the Web 
at a place called It’s an experi- 
ment that could change the way we watch 
television in the fast-approaching Broad- 
band Era. And it’s the stuff that new-media 
dreams are made of. But is Pseudo the real deal?
