ONE MINUTE!" SHOUTS THE YOUNG WOMAN WITH 

--  theclipboard, a headset clamped over her dreadlocks. A 
thirtyish guy with a ponytail perches on a stepladder 
beside her, messing with a floodlight up near the black 
tinplate ceiling. A fat-boy rapper stands behind a pair 
ofturntables and two giant speakersat the opposite side 

of this makeshift TV studio, where hand-painted scenery 
replicates a New York City subway station. “Twenty seconds!” 

People stop talking, but the old wooden floorboards still creak 
loudly as visitors jockey for position, angling for a view. Two 
camera operators are standing by. 

“Three...two.,.one!” 

And we're live with 88 HIP-HOP, a music show emanating every 
Wednesday night from this partially renovated space operated by 
PsEupO, the first and largest provider of live video content for the 
Web. Headquartered in Manhattan on lower Broadway (sometimes 
called Silicon Alley South), Pseudo Netcasts almost 40 hours oflive 
programming each week, witha suite of shows touching on every- 
thing from music, video games, and professional wrestling to space 
exploration, digital culture, and poetry. 

The ultralow costof distributing Net video has encouraged upstarts 
such as Pseudo to chase multiple niche audiences—like cable tele- 
vision, only cheaper. The future could be much bigger, since Net- 
casting has the potential to reach a global audience of hundreds of 
millions. Some estimates predict that by 2005 more than 16 million 
Web surfers in the U.S. alone will have the high-speed access that 
makes Net TV a practical proposition rather than a high-tech stunt. 

Pseudo leads the pack with more experience, more hours online 
each week, more employees, and a team of sometimes wild but 
always memorable and highly informed personalities presenting 


the shows. It’s an online experiment that runs the gamut—from | 


the crazy, sexy cool of TanyaTV to the straight sports talk of Offthe | 
Field,on the recently launched Q.B. Club Channel. Inabitofa coup, | 


PSEUDO’S AN EXPERIMENT THAT COULD POINT THE WAY TO OUR CONTENT FUTURE 


Pseudo also just scored press credentials for the major political | 
party conventions this summer. It’san experiment that could point 
the way to our content future—but like all experiments, it entails 
risk. Even when broadband becomes more everyday reality than 
buzzword, no one knows how many people will really want to 
watch TV via the Net. There may bea reason why text-heavy sites 
like Yahoo!, AOL, and Lycos consistently dominate online. 

Pseudo could turn out to be a failed ego trip taken by a bunch of 
kids burning through other people’s money. That is one distinct 
possibility. Still, on the Net, being first and building a lead is often 
the crucial difference—witness the dominance of Amazon.com and 
eBay. And Pseudo's CEO, David Bohrman, is adamant that the magic 
ingredient of interactivity will make Net TV a radically different 
and irresistible medium. “We've all been yakking about interac- 
tive television for years,” he says. “Pseudo can do this!” 

Right now Pseudo measures its audience in hundreds of thou- | 
sands, not millions, and viewers with 56Kbps modems often have | 
to squint at jerky, blurry images while listening to audio that 
sounds like a cheap digital answering machine. Pseudo also faces 
a growing army of competitors (see “Must-See Net TV,” page 109), 
such as ENTERTAINDOM, where you can find clips of ’N Sync | 
and clever animations that circumvent the limitations of full- 
motion video. However, one high-profile rival, DEN: DIGITAL 
ENTERTAINMENT NETWORK, ran out of money in mid-May. 


Anda threat even more ominous looms further ahead: Media 
giants may wait until the field matures, figure out what works, 
then move in to eat the competition. Bohrman is acutely aware 
of this. “I think we have the next year and a half to set the stan- 
dards of design, while the networks figure it out and AOL Time 
Warner gets its act together,” he says. “If we haven't built Pseudo 
into a force by then, we're likely to be overwhelmed.” 


S LAUNCHED AN-TURNED- 
millionaire Joshua Harris, as a company that man- 
aged and moderated chat for the online service 
Prodigy. Several years earlier, he had founded Jupiter 
Communications, an Internet research and con- 
sulting business, in his apartment. But once he had 
created that hugely successful enterprise, Harris moved 
on. “I watch the basics go up,” he says. “After that, I don’t 

find running a company very interesting.” 

Harris claims that his track record with Jupiter would have 
enabled him to get money for almost any Net venture, which is 
probably true. Still, he says, “I wasn’t interested in a quick kill, 
which could have been easy. I was interested in the entertainment 
business and creating a brand with staying power. At this point, 
Pseudo is on the brink of being a brand name.” 

In 1999, having run Pseudo for five years, Harris followed his pat- 


106 JuLy 2000 YAHOO! INTERNET LIFE 


tern of stepping down once the fundamentals are in place. He quit 
as Pseudo’s CEO, retaining the title of chairman but spending only 
afew hoursa weekat the office. He says he has absolute confidence 
in Bohrman, who took over as CEO in January. 

The power shuffle seemed calculated to soothe investors who 
might have been spooked by Harris's outré style. Harris is a some- 
time New York performance artist with a subversive sense of humor. 
He's been known to dress in clown makeup and yellow rubber 
gloves, call himself“Luvvy,” and orchestrate bizarre parties like his 
monthlong millennium blowout. He describes himself as a media- 
world manifestation of Big Brother and isinvestinga lot ofhisown 
time and considerable wealth working toward a future in which 
all of us—beginning with himself, his friends, and unsuspecting 


guests—are on-camera all the time. Visionary? Possibly, yet it’s | 


enough to make some investors nervous. 

Bohrman, with a neatly trimmed beard anda receding hairline, 
is two decades older and 20 pounds heavier than the Silicon Alley 
cats in Pseudo’s control room. He has serious old-media chops, 
with credentials that include stints as executive vice president 
of CNN’s Financial News Network, executive producer respon- 
sible for all breaking news at NBC News, and senior producer of 
ABC's Nightline. He is unlikely to dona clown suit, but this “adult 
in the room’” (as older CEOs of young startups are affectionately 
called) is more than willing to get down and dirty in the Pseudo 
studio. “The most compelling thing about Pseudo is the fertile 
creative minds,” he says. “This is the place to create and build. 
There's very little creative, very little new, in television.” 


AS 88 HIP-HOP GOES ON THE AIR—OR RATHER, THE 

Net—its producer, Randy Nkonoki-Ward, remains 

hands-off, undisturbed by the chaos of production 

staffers bumping into visitors, who in turn are trip- 

ping over cables. Nkonoki-Ward isa skinny guy in his 

twenties with a brilliant smile and a haircut barely 

longer than stubble. Formerly a supervisor of a con- 

struction company, he says he was “making more money 

than I had ever made, but it was like building my own prison. I 
was ready to go postal. So I quit.” 

After turning in his hard hat, Nkonoki-Wardstarteda recordcom- 
pany witha partner and rented a 900-number ina desperate bid to 
get exposure. “You dialed 1-900-88-HIPHOP,” he explains. “‘Press 1 
for an interview witha celebrity guest; press 2 for an up-and-com- 
ing artist'—and there was music, the phat beat of the week.” 

He recorded the shows in his bedroom but was driven deep into 
debt by the cost of advertising, record production, demo tapes, and 
the 900-service. Then he got lucky. One of his tapes reached 
Pseudo, and Nkonoki-Ward started drawing a paycheck instead of 
blowing his cash on an expensive hobby. In turn, he became a 
believer: “There’s absolutely nothing like it on television.” 

Nkonoki-Ward’s zeal is shared by most of the twentysomething 
producers at Pseudo. Jess Zaino runs a celebrity gossip program 
titled StarFreaky, featuring herself as a wide-eyed, breathless fan. At 
19, Zaino dropped out of college in California, came to New York, 
and crashed a party where Harris was a guest. Zaino recalls that she 
almost drank herself into a stupor before proclaiming she'd “lick 


the floors to work here.” The next day she was rewarded with a job 
as receptionist; eight months later she started producing her show. 

On her program, Zaino exercises her party-crashing skills in a 
relentless quest to ambush celebrities in New York and Holly- 
wood (she crashed the Oscars this year). “They talk to me because 
Ilook so young and!’m this cute little girl,” she says, with a squeaky 
giggle. “I want StarFreaky to build; 1 want more celebrities on the 
show. And I want the people just to hang out and have fun with 
me. I want to be on TV, in movies—I really feel like I can bea per- 
sonality out there.” 

Derek Gordon is another starstruck Pseudo producer, hosting 
...And Justice for Brawl, a weekly wrestling talk show that has fea- 
tured guests such as Bret “The Hit Man” Hart. On-camera, Gordon 
renames himself “Die Hard” and wears a satin cape, sun- 
glasses, and leather pants that he says cost $600. “I'm working in 
character,” he explains, with intense seriousness. 

Die Hard was a Brooklyn wrestling fan who became a small-time 


| manager in 1991. Like Nkonoki-Ward, he promoted himself with 
| a900-number until he persuaded Pseudo to give him his ownshow, 
| in which he delivers commentary in the relentless, open-throated 
| shout that’s mandatory for anyone in the wrestling business. 


These programs share a common trait: They're run by young, 


| hard-core fans who are trying to reach others like themselves. 


While each night at 6:00 the network news airs, the grandiosely 
titled All Games Network arrives on your browser, featuring reviews 
illustrated with clips from recent video games. This isnot what reg- 


| ularly scheduled programming looks like for most of America. For 
| some, All Gamesisa salvation, a parallel universe just a click away. 


The All Games production offices, one floor below the Pseudo 
studio, are a hastily improvised shambles. Most walls are grimy

88 HIP-HOP—interactive music, interviews, and live performance. 


Microsoft Media Player and

it’s sent out in two video formats, 
one of them tiny (176 x 132 pix-

His show has been sponsored by game manufacturers, but he claims 


he doesn’t soften his critiques for them. Clearly, though, the Web's | 
proven ability to sell, sell, sell must create some conflicts of inter- | 


est. When a manufacturer pays fora bannerad, begging you to click 
and buy the game that’s being reviewed—it really won’tcomplain 
if Rubin tells his audience the game is a turkey? 

This April, Pseudo’s relationship with its sponsors became even 
more intimate when it offered such things as downloadable out- 
takes and promotional e-mail for the movie American Psycho. In 
the words of Jeanne Meyer, Pseudo’s senior vice president for mar- 


_____ high-speed Net access. Some 
video streams are broadcast from a live server, while others go 
through local servers, including Intervu, an online video distrib- 
ution service. High traffic can impose a delay as long 
as two minutes. 

The bottom line: You’re watching a program and decide to 
send a message to the guys at the studio. By the time they 
receive it, they could be discussing a different topic altogether. 
A conventional TV show with a computer receiving e-mail is 
far better suited for real-time interactivity (CNN’s Talkback- 
Live! already scrolls viewer e-mail at the bottom of the screen). 


‘WE NEED INTERACTION AMONG THE COMMUNITY OF VIEWERS AND WITH THE PROGRAM’ 


keting: “Our content has got value to a lot of people who want to 
use it to enhance the buying experience. And we get a cut of that 
revenue.” Inconventional media, that kind of thing would be high- 
ly controversial. Imagine, for example,a TV network running a doc- 
umentary on footwear with a Nike icon and an 800-number in the 
corner of the screen, and getting a kickback every time a viewer 
called to buy a new pair of sneakers. If Pseudo takes this path, it’s 
just a short distance to the Home Shopping Network. 


DURING NORMAL BUSINESS HOURS, DAVID BOHRMAN 
works in Pseudo’s corporate offices overlooking Broad- 
way. It'sa freshly renovated space with pure-white walls, 
like an art gallery’s. Wearing a button-down shirt but 
no jacket or tie, he leans back in his chair behind the 
| big new Apple flat-screen monitor that sits atop his mas- 
sive modern desk. He doesn’t dispute that much of 
Pseudo’s programming has the familiar look of regular tele- 
vision. “But that’s not the way it’s going to be,” he says. “We need 
interaction among the community of viewers and with the program. 
And we need information next to the player but not part of it.” 

To enable those things to come about, Pseudo’s own media 
player supports a separate text window, which Bohrman wants 
to use as a counterpoint to the talking heads: “It could say, ‘This 
isJohn Smith. Here’s the book he’s talking about. And by the way, 
here’s how to buy the book. And you can send him e-mail if you 
think he’s an asshole.’ I mean, why not?” 


“There area host of problems,” Bohrman concedes. “But [they'll] 
get fixed. And [when] everything works perfectly, the metaphor, 
the interface, what-this-is, will have been set.” This remains his 
central focus—defining a totally new medium before anyone else 
gets there. The 18 months that Bohrman has allowed himself 
before the heavy hitters arrive sounds barely long enough. 

At the same time, this development period will be costly. With 
50,000 square feet of rented space and more than 200 full-time 
employees, Pseudo could be looking at expenses of $500,000 a 
month. And with some ofits shows reaping as few as 3,000 hits while 
they're going out live, how can the company stay solvent fora year 
anda half? (Pseudo, for its part, claims many additional page views 
after each broadcast.) Although Pseudo’s eccentric, high-energy cul- 
ture appears to be the model ofa new-media shop, it has had to face 
welcome-to-the-corporate-world hurdles, such as a sexual harass- 
ment suit brought by a former employee this January. 

Jupiter Communications, which owes its success to founder 
Joshua Harris, now offers askeptical analysis of Pseudo’s future. 
“Whether and when they will make money is a big mystery,” 
says David Card, a senior analyst. “Video content online is a 
pretty crummy experience. I think their situation is very chal- 
lenging. I don’t know how long they can last. Audiences will 
be small—maybe for a while, and maybe forever.” 

Bohrman, however, is unperturbed. “There’s plenty of money 
out there,” he says with a shrug. “In fact, we can begin to be 
picky about funding.”

_OF A NAPSTER FANATIC 


| ae, Se uncontrolled descent into the underworld of free music 


el tenes ho 


fa. SLOCS ie ei Cses ieac Etsy OF 


110 suULY 2000 YAHOO! INTERNET LIFE 


Pid. LOUGHRAN 


P3. SURE, | HAD HEARD THE TERM THOUSANDS 
of times, but I still didn’t quite get it. CD- 
quality sound on my PC? Great, but I didn’t 
have a “ripper,” or a “burner,” ora Rio player, 
or whatever the hell it was you needed for it. 

Thad visited MP3.com, but the experience was less than eye- 
opening. After an hour of sampling the free downloads, I had 
decided that I'm not patient enough to wade through a pool 
of bad garage bands. 

Then a friend mentioned that he had 200 songs on his hard 
drive. This was back when 200 seemed like a huge number. 
And his ability to acquire 200 songs from an amateur-night 
site like MP3.com indicated a stupendous lack of taste. 

“Two hundred songs?” I said. “All of them from MP3.com?” 

“No way,” he replied. And then he said the word: Napster. 

I'd heard a lot of buzz about NAPSTER, but again I was igno- 
rant. Didn't] have to pay? Buy some special hardware? Would 
I catch a virus? Be arrested? None of the above, my buddy 
assured me. Minutes later 1 had downloaded and installed the 
latest Napster 2.0 beta and was “online, sharing 0 files.” 

I must pause at this point, for a tear is welling in my eye. 
Remembering the first time you used Napster is like remem- 
bering your first kiss. It’s a quantum moment that surpasses 
your experience to that point and takes your understanding 
of the world to a new level. Suddenly, you're a part of some 
club that you've always wanted to get into, and once you're 
in, you find that it’s even better than advertised. 

Where would I start? | tried to think ofa tune I hadn't heard 
inawhile, something that would transport me back toa favorite 
era. thought of those early MTV days—back when a few min- 
utes of unpolished animation counted as a video, back when 
what mattered on the Music Television network was the music. 

Tom Tom Club, | typed. “Genius of Love.” 

The display filled up: “Search returned 23 results.” Wow. I 
could even pick through and find the particular version that 
I wanted (the long mix, of course). I double-clicked on the 
title, and three minutes later it was there: “Genius of Love,” by 
the Tom Tom Club, sounding as much like a CD as my tinny 
computer speakers could manage. It was that easy. 

At first, I tried to be selective about my use of Napster. I was 
going to download only those songs that I would never buy, 
those little masterpiece singles of yesteryear that, no matter 
how good, didn’t justify purchasing the whole album. [grabbed 
another MTV favorite, “Paid in Full (Seven Minutes of Mad- 
ness—The Coldcut Remix),” by Eric B. and Rakim. Then two 
more songs that recall my youth: “Your Love,” by the Outfield, 
and “Only Time Will Tell,” by Asia. Then “Just What I Need- 
ed,” by the Cars—the one Ric Ocasek vocal performance I can 
bear. Ditto for “You Don’t Know How It Feels,” by Tom Petty. I 
then pulled down “Fool’s Gold,” the tune that justified the exis- 
tence of the Stone Roses. Deee-Lite’s “Groove Is in the Heart.” 
“Cannonball,” by the Breeders. And for a change of pace, I 
grabbed “Mas Que Nada,” by Sergio Mendes and Brasil ’66. 

I was a sweepstakes winner on a shopping spree, careering 
wildly among the racks and bins in the record store in my head. 
Nick Lowe’s “Cruel to Be Kind.” Kool Moe Dee’s “Wild Wild 
West.” Jim Carroll’s “People Who Died.” Stevie Wonder’s “Pas- 


time Paradise.” Blur’s “Song 2.” Rancid’s “Ruby Soho.” Joe Jack- 
son’s “Breaking Us in Two.” Golden Earring’s “Radar Love.” 
LL Cool J’s “Mama Said Knock You Out.” 

Thad come, I thought, to the end of this scandalous orgy of 
downloading, and I saw that I had something like 30 songs. 
The songs are big files (one minute of MP3 music gobbles up 
about 1MB), and I was feeling a little nervous about having 
that much contraband on my employer's computer. 

That blip of uneasiness passed as I remembered an obscure 
but significant musical moment in a favorite film. It’s from 
National Lampoon's European Vacation, when the Griswolds go 
through the entire Louvre in halfan hour. The frantic sequence 
is accompanied by some spastic Euro-pop that sounds like 
the Beach Boys gone punk—in French, 

I needed that song. 

I went to the Internet Movie Database, looked up the film, 
clicked the Soundtrack Listing link, and scrolled down to find 
“Ga Plane Pour Moi,” by Plastic Bertrand. Plugged it into the 
Napster search engine, and it was there. [couldn't believe some- 
one actually had this song. I clicked to download. 

But no luck. I got the cursed “remotely queued” message— 
that’s Napster for “No song for you!” Damn. 

By now it was 11 pM., and whatever I had stayed late at the 
office to do had not been done. 1 put in about halfan hourmore 
of work, then decided to call ita night. But before I turned off 
my computer, I logged on to Napster again and ran another 
search for that French song. 

Again, itwas there. And this time it belonged tosomeone with 
a better connection. Within minutes, it was mine. And this 
was when I learned the terrible, terrible secret of Napster. 
Unlike a store’s or a catalog’s, Napster’s inventory is in con- 
stant flux. There is always the chance, however small, that if 
you just log on often enough, some weirdo out there will have 
the song you want. It’s like snowflakes, a box of chocolates, 
and Halloween all rolled into one. 

I was in a French state of mind and decided to pull down 
the entirety of Air’s first album, which I'd wanted for some 
time. Done. This set me to thinking about hip bar music and’ 
that Moby album I'd heard was so good. Done. Then I remem- 
bered the Everything but the Girl CD I love so much but had 
leftatmy parents’ house in Virginia. Done. AndI had read good 
things about Beth Orton. 

Before I knew it, I had expanded the exceptions to my Nap- 
ster rule. According to my brand-new code of Napster conduct, 
I would now download only 

1) one-off singles from albums I knew I'd never buy; 

2) material I had actually owned but had lost; 

3) new stuff, but only in the “try before you buy” spirit. 

Inessence, I resolved to download only things I couldn't play 
from a CD I already owned. You must have your principles. 

It was 2 AM, and I now had 120 songs. I was searching for 
“Masoko Tanga,” by the Police (this song fell under Exception 
No. 2 because of a scratch on my beloved Outlandos D'Amour 
CD), and I was coming up dry. Time to go home. 

When I got to work the next day, I checked for “Masoko 
Tanga.” No dice. I checked again at lunch. Nope. When I 
logged in at the end of the day, the song still wasn’t there,

but seeing as I was in no particular rush to get home, I pulled 
down the excellent Sparklehorse album I had somehow lost. 
Before I left, I got some old favorites by the Smiths and the Cure. | 
Technically, these were acquired in violation of my self-imposed 
Napster ethic, but I decided to reward myself for having the dis- 
cipline to follow those rules while building a music collection 
that now approached 200 tracks. After one more fruitless search 
for “Masoko Tanga,” I went home at 10:30. 

During the next few days, I kept checking for “Masoko Tanga” 
withoutsuccess. In the meantime, | picked up music ranging from 


the weak Synchronicity stuff knew I would never listen to. I want- 
ed one song; I got 78. I felt shame at having been rabid enough to 
downloada four-CD boxed set. What,I thought, is happening tome? 

I didn't worry about it for too long, though, because I remem- 
bered the Bob Marley boxed set my penniless screenwriter friend 
out in L.A. keeps “forgetting” to mail back to me. 

This, my friends, is how you amass a collection of 600 MP3 files. 
And as you plunge deeper into addiction, the things you do for 
hard-disk space become more and more depraved. I deletedall the 
dumb little movies my friends had e-mailed me. I zapped all

the new and different (Pizzicato Five, Bjérk, Cornershop) to the 
oldand nearly forgotten (Madness, Ice-T, Bill Monroe). And thanks 
to the Similar Artists feature of the ALL MUSIC GUIDE, | 
no song existed in a vacuum; everyplace was a gate- 
way to someplace else (kinda like the Web itself). I 
tended to drift back in time from the most modern 
of music to legends of (gulp) easy-listening and 
lounge music. Stereolab led me back to Esquivel. Air 
led me back to Burt Bacharach. And that old Sergio 
Mendes song led me, alas, to more old Sergio Mendes. 

All the while, I never saw “Masoko Tanga.” Late one evening, 
when I had just about given up hope...it was there. I clicked it to 
download. Then I began to wonder about the kindred loser who 
had this remarkably unpopular song. I chose the Add User to Hot | 
List function, which lets you see a person's entire catalog. This | 
guy had MP3s of the Message in a Box collection—four CDs con- 
taining all 78 songs the Police had recorded—for anyone to copy. 

Did I want all 78 songs the Police had recorded? I didn’t even 
ask myself this question. The important thing was that it was there, 
and if I acted now, I could have it all. I downloaded the lot, even 


nonessential applications. The saddest moment of all was the 
night I uninstalled Quake II, Duke Nukem, and Battlezone. 

Why this consuming passion for MP3s? I’m no digital shrink, 
but I’m sure that one thing driving my music jones is the possi- 
bility that Napster won't be around too much longer. Napster; its 
open-source equivalent, GNUTELLA; and MP3.com are on shaky 
legal ground. By press time they could be shut down. I don't doubt 
that the music will be out there, and free, for those who are savvy 
enough to find it. I just doubt that it will ever again be this easy. 

The pie chart of my hard-disk diagram says it all. The Used Space 
monster, likea relentless purple Pac-Man, is closing its jaws around 
the sliver of pink Free Space. Every day, I see a little less of it. Every 
day, my applications run just a little slower. Like my departed 
heroes—John Belushi, Kurt Cobain, Keith Moon (note to self: get 
“Substitute”)—I am out of control and headed for a crash. 

Now you'll have to excuse me. I've got to run another search for 
“T Will Not Fall,” by Wire Train. 


AUTHOR'S NOTE: No Metallica tunes were downloaded in the making 
of this piece.

12G July 2000 YAHOO! INTERNET LIFE 


WWW.YIL.COM {27 


ALK ABOUT EASY MONEY. CYBERBEGGING IS ; 

booming—andit’s a cinch. Just log on, ham it up, and 

rake it in. Never before have vagabonds ventured 

beyond the streets to cash in with such ease and élan. 

Moving online, panhandlers have cornered a whole 
new market: a World Wide Web saturated with suckers. 

Just a decade ago, media-savvy beggars had to shell out as much 
as $100 for a classified newspaper ad asking for a buck. Today, 
cybervags can get online and on their knees at virtually no charge. 
Lazy lounge-chair enthusiasts have never had it so good. Begging 
used to be down and dirty. Now, for cyber do-nothings, it’s online, 
clean, and fun. Tap outa few lines of HTML and you're good to go. 
Just watch the money pour in. | 

Got no shame? It’s not too late to get in on the action. Join the 
legion of wired alms-seekers offering nothing more than their 
appreciation for surfers’ donated dollars. Here’s the equation: 
Generous Netizens pay. You, the beggar, receive. Doubters, check 
it out for yourselves. In less than a year, THE SOCIETY TO PREVENT 
MY EMPLOYMENT has earned its owner (who calls herself Princess 
Natalie) a tidy $453. It probably doesn't hurt that her flowing 


Begging used to be down 
and dirty. Now, for cyber 
do-nothings, it’s online, clean, 
and fUlM. Tap out a few lines of | 


HTML and you're good to go 


locks and prom queen looks are featured on the site. (You never 
know though. Maybe the pic is just some greasy Joe’s favorite 
download, and Princess Natalie is actually a 60-year-old frumpy 
lump.) But lest you fear that only a pretty face can pull in the 
dough, surf to Rich Schmidt's THE AMAZING “SEND ME A DOLLAR” 
WEB SITE, which features no photos whatsoever. Schmidt, the | 
granddaddy of Web beggars, has pulled in more than $1,000. | 

How do these pleaders do it? Master mendicants report that | 
people love getting nothing for something: All you have to do 
is ask. Schmidt claims to have received money from 800 people 
in Europe, Australia, Asia, and North America. “My original 
goal was to geta dollar a day for the next million days, but then 
I did the math and realized that would take over 2,000 years,” 
Schmidt says with disappointment. 

When asking doesn’t work, some try demanding dough. “Give 
me money,” insists Misha Glouberman’s GIVE ME MONEY: THE 
USELESS WEB SITE You PAY FoR. KC Hagemann’s SEND ME A 
DOLLAR site takes a similar tack. “You NEED to send mea dollar,” 
writes Hagemann, who moonlights at Dairy Queen when he’s 
not carving outa career as an online beggar. As you might expect, 
neither reports great success. Glouberman admits that the com- 
pany that once managed his online receipts has long since gone 
out of business, though hestill urges visitors to request his address _| 
and mail him a check. “I don’t beg,” says Glouberman proudly. | 


pag the 


Although online beggars may seem like renegades, | 
they do follow a set of unwritten rules. Here are 
the five basic laws of cyberbegging: | 


Be funny. If you're after hits, nothing will spread the word 
like laughs. And when people laugh, they're happy. When 
they're happy, their purse strings loosen. Bingo. 


If you're not going to be funny, at least be pathetic. “Hon- 
estly I don't think that anyone, not one person, will send 
me one dollar,” moans a sorry character at PLEASE SEND 
ME MONEY!!! [members.tripod.com/cgilley24/money. 
htm] who has learned to capitalize on his wretchedness, 
“Someone, please prove me wrong.” 


Make no promises. If there's one thing that turns off poten- 
tial donors, it's the implication that they might get some- 
thing decent in return. They don’t want you to come over 
and clean their windshield or sing show tunes off-key. 
Nothing seems to work better for online beggars than 
emphasizing that this is a one-way street. | 


Ifyou must show gratitude, be original. Offer to send ames- 
sage of thanks ona balloon into an outer galaxy. Or to make 
your next birthday wish a prayer for your donor's health. 
Whatever you do, make it different, memorable, ridiculous. 


Get greedy. Don't sit back and gloat over a $1 donation. 
Take, for example, Reynolds Kosloskey, webmaster of 
Save the Suburbanites, who says, “One of these days, some 
bored multimillionaire might just decide to send us away 
on our million-dollar worldwide trip.” That's the spirit— 
go for the gold.

Rate-a-Beggar 


Rich Schmidt, host of the Amazing “Send Me a Dol- 
lar” Web Site, has made a hobby of keeping an eye 
on his rivals. Below, he assesses the field. Dollar signs 
($) denote how well his fellow freeloaders have fared, 
according to Schmidt's highly subjective criteria. 


THE SOCIETY TO PREVENT 

MY EMPLOYMENT 
[egomania.nu/causes/indexsoc.html] 
“Here's a feisty little site run by Princess Nata- 
lie. It has wit, charm, and just the right amount 
of attitude. This could be my only real compe- 
tition out there... hope she gives me the e-mail 
addresses of the two guys who sent her $50 
and $60 in exchange for this plug!” 

S$$$$ 


MAKE ME RICHER THAN BILL GATES 
[web2.airmail.net/gandolf/you.htm] 
“Love the title of this one, and some of the text 
is actually funny. Try this one out: ‘When you 
“Make Me Richer Than Bill Gates” you are also 
helping the global community by allowing me 
to travel to foreign countries and exotic loca- 
tions. Thereby boosting the economy of the 
struggling nations that | visit.’ By the way, this 
site plays the same obnoxious musical loop 
over and over till it drives you mad!” 


$$$ 


THE VIRTUAL BUM 

[xx.net/virtual-bum] 

“Takes a long time to load a large graphic of an economically 
challenged transient sitting in an alley....This site is also a tad 
interactive because you can help him select how he will spend 
your dough. The three choices are food, booze, or hookers. We 
found this site to be politically incorrect.” 

$$$ 


CYBER BEGGAR 2000 

[cyberbeggar.virtualave.net] 

“Our first international listing! This page originates from Russia, 
where capitalism now apparently runs rampant... The graphics 
are weak, but I give him credit for trying. Check it out!” 


$4 


A FRIEND IN NEED IS A FRIEND INDEED! 
[www.fastlane.net/~sandman/help] 

“Sets the tone with some line art of a begging boy straight 
outta some Dickens novel. A straight-ahead plea for help. You'll 
have to be the judge if you think it’s legit or not. | don’t need 
the extra negative karma.” 


St 


Taking Glouberman's disrespect for site visitors one step fur- 
ther is THE B. FATT & LAZY SCAM! “Shut the f—k up and keep your 
questions to yourself,” the site’s self-described scam page barks. 
If Mr. Fatt is to be believed, he and his colleague Lazy have bam- 
boozled surfers to the tune of $338 to date. There goes the polite- 
ness-is-best theory. But even the nastiest beggars back off in the 
end: After resolutely demanding your contribution, Glouberman 
admits that he won’t be offering anything in return. And Messrs. 
Fatt and Lazy do show a token of gratitude. “We really can’t thank 
you enough, old chap. It’s quite smashing of you indeed. You 
won't regret it. We love you. Blah blah blah. Blah blah blah.” 

For some, posting a gimme site is easy. Glouberman spent one 
evening and virtually no cash putting his up. For others, it’s taxing. 
Schmidt says he spends up to 
five hours a week working on 
hissiteand$80a year fora post 
office box. “Ruthlessly begging 
for money can be pretty hard 
work,” says Reynolds Koslos- 
key, webmaster for SAVE THE 
SUBURBANITES. Maybe so, but 
at least he never gets rained on. 

Begging online could open 
up new windows of opportu- 
nity for the average street pan- 
handler. “Beggars might as 
well step into the 21st centu- 
ry,” Schmidt says, “so they can 
access 250 million people 
instead of the 100 or so they 
see on the corner every day.” 
Rather than filling up a coffee 
cup with jangling coins, you 
can fill up your bank account with cash-by-mail—just by selling 
your sob story. The self-proclaimed BITTER AMERICAN CYBER BUM 
pleads that he desperately needs to take his kids to Disney World. 
No matter that he has ajoband earnsa salary. The plea’s the thing. 

Only in America, you might be thinking? Nope. One of the 
sites “reviewed” on Schmidt's Rate-a-Beggar page (excerpted at left) 
is based in Russia. Online importuning is universal. Okay, so none 
of the Web’s new breed of beggars has taken in seven digits— 
maybe it’s just a matter of time. 

If you do decide to beg online, don’t expect respect any more 
than you would offline. In March, Princess Natalie was named an 
official dork at DORK OF THE DaY.com. Andsurfers offer even stern- 
er rebukes, barely printable in this magazine. “You're a bleating 
foal,” one reader proclaimed to the princess, “a curdled staggering 
mutant...smeared richly with the effluvia and offal accompany- 
ing your alleged birth into this world.” Tough crowd. 

Butit’s not those angry digerati that entreaters are after. It’s the 
mom and pop who laugh at their gumption and toss a few bucks 
their way in gratitude. Or the teen prankster who shares his 
allowance to applaud a plot he wishes he had hatched. So what 
are you doing daydreaming about an e-business that will make 
yourich? There's a much easier way to earna fortune on the Inter- 
net. Get out there and beg. Remember: All it takes is a buck a 
day...for a million days. 


JEREMY CAPLAN is an associate editor of Yahoo! Internet Life.

Meditation

{ itation is a means to achieve control 


over our chattering minds. The sim- 
plest and most effective technique 
was practiced by the Buddha himself: 
vipassana, “to see things as they real- 
ly are.” Read the Code of Discipline 
for a detailed description, then follow 
links to individual meditation centers 
around the world—which offer free 
courses. You can print out an appli- 
cation or apply online. [dhamma.org]

QUTS7 MATTERS? BY DEAN FOUST 
Lis me 


Fa 


NTIL VERY RECENTLY, MEETING FACE-TO- 
face witha backslapping insurance agent 
was a better bet than purchasing a poli- 
cy over the Net. Quotes provided by sites 
for comparison shopping weren't always 
binding, and most were just guesstimates 
based on the insurer's regulatory filings. 
What's more, the law required that you print out 
an application, sign it, and mail it in before the 
policy was approved. Even then, the savings were 
usually negligible because of state laws requir- 
ing insurers to charge the same for all policies. 

Now there’s more reason to start browsing for 
coverage. For openers, sites like INSWEB [insweb. 
com] and QUICKEN.COM: INSURANCE [quicken. 
com/insurance] are working more closely with 
insurers to make sure that the quotes you get 
are accurate. And thanks to regulators’ growing 
acceptance of e-signatures, insurers are start- 
ing to close deals on the Web. 

In early April, InsWeb began allowing Cali- 
fornia drivers to buy auto coverage online; the 
company plans to expand the service to other 
states over the coming year. The trick: While 
you're filling out the e-application, the site is

keting officer for InsWeb. “It appeals to some-

LTHOUGH HARD-CORE LUDDITES MIGHT 
beg to differ, it seems safe to say that the 
Internet will not destroy high culture 
after all. # This year marked the begin- 
ning of a fourth decade for PROJECT 
GUTENBERG [gutenberg.net], the progenitor 
4 of the many efforts to digitize the world’s 
great books. Now on the Web, the project archives 
texts in the public domain—those whose copy- 
right protections have expired—including the 
work ofliterary icons suchas Shakespeare, Lewis 
Carroll,and Edgar Allan Poe. As of March, the site 
had amassed roughly a quarter of the 10,000 books 
ithopes to have inits database by the end of 2001. 
Beyond cultural conservation, sucha giant data- 
base changes our relationship to the books. Texts 
can be searched by keyword or studied and com- 
pared in ways not possible with printed matter. 
Another leading e-library, BARTLEBY.COM, 
houses titles such as H.G. Wells’s The Time 
Machine and W.E.B. DuBois’s The Souls of Black 
Folk, along with a slew of reference tomes. The 


ABOVE: Mark Twain's wit is alive on the Web. 
RIGHT: The Hubble Space Telescope in orbit. 


The Text Big Thing 


From classics to oddities, old books are finding new 


life online 


site lets users do very specific searches, such as 
looking for all occurrences of the word Brooklyn 
in Walt Whitman's Leaves of Grass. For the idle 
and curious, it’sa great diversion; forstudents and 
professional researchers, it can be a lifesaver. 
Not everyone who follows in Project Guten- 
berg’s footsteps does so out of a desire to save 


“classic” works. Since there are no rights hold- } 


ers to pay and the cost of putting texts online 


is negligible, even books that are not so great ; © ; 
! Astronomy for Kids! Budding 


can be resurrected. Witness THE NAKED WORD 
[crosswinds.net/~freeebooks/nakedword], a public 


domain site produced by a lone, self-taught pro- ; 


grammer witha passion for obscure, 100-year-old 
literature. Among the odd- 
ities archived here are mem- 
oirs from the wife of General 
George Custer and a subpar, 
little-known shortstory written late 
in Mark Twain's career. It might seem strange 
to preserve these things, but when you're 
“keeping the past alive—one book at a 
time,” the Naked Word’s motto, there’s 
noroom forsnobbery. A little more 
storage is only a Zip disk away.

ONITOR STOCKS IN REAL TIME. TEACH 
yourself how to use an abacus. Type 
secret messages to coworkers during 
ameeting. Thanks to software avail- 
able for the ubiquitous PalmPilot, 

youcan make your handheld do just about 
anything—even turn intoa digital compass 
or aremote control for your TV. 

Want to get your palms on Palm soft- 
ware? At the company’s official Web site 
[palm.com], users can check out Palm Inc.’s 
favorite picks. To view them, click the Prod- 
ucts and Software link, then scroll down 
and click Platinum Solutions. 

Although the Palm Web site does point 
users to some great software, its selection 
lacks breadth. In truth, PALM GEAR H.Q. 
[palmgear.com] is home to the best range 
of software. This amazing PDA hub rates 


Looking to spice up your PalmPilot? Sites devoted to the popular 
handheld can guide you to the hottest PDA software on the market 


the newest programs available and lists its 
Top 50 Downloads. 

Other popularsoftware repositories, such 
as CNET DOWNLOAD.COM [download.com] 
and ZDNET: DOWNLOADS [hotfiles.com], can 
also help you find Palm programs. But you 
may have more fun perusing the multitude 
ofsoftware available at any of the more than 
700sites making up the THE PALMPILOT WEB 
RING [webring.org/cgi-bin/webring?list& 
ring=geoff]. 

Ofcourse, the PalmPilotisn’t the only PDA 
onthe market. The Handspring Visor, which 
runs on the Palm OS, is wildly popular and 
has its own software hub, viSORCENTRAL 
[visorcentral.com]. HANDANGO [handango. 
com], another online repository for hand- 
held software, includes programs for Win- 
dows CE-powered devices.

QUICKTIPS 


PUBLIC ACCESS 

Show me yours and I'll show you mine. At 
BACKFLIP [backflip.com], surfers can post 
Netscape Navigator bookmarks or Microsoft 
Internet Explorer favorites to the Web, sharing 
them with the world. The free service helps 
you build a Yahoo!-like directory for your link 
collection and offers a useful search feature for 
finding sites by keyword. Your personal 
account is password-protected and can be 
accessed from any Net-enabled computer. 
CLICKMARKS.COM is another excellent Web- 
based bookmarking service. 


QUICK ESCAPE 

Your boss walks into the office just as you're 
wrapping up an online game of mah-jongg. 
You fumble for your mouse and attempt to 
close the browser window. Instead, you 
misclick and maximize the screen. Kiss 

that bonus good-bye. Next time, just click 
(Windows key + D] to close all open windows. 
Other useful PC keyboard combos include 
[Ctrl + H] (open the History window) and 
Tab] (move to the next link or text field),

CUTTING THE CORD 


Wondering how your name and phone num- 
ber landed on every telemarketing list in 
America? It’s a mystery, but, mercifully, you 
can put an end to the calls. At JUNKBUSTERS 
[junkbusters.com], surfers can find out 
how to get off lists for good and learn 

tactics to protect their privacy. And at 
ESSENTIAL.COM you can sign up fora free 
service called Teleguard, which automatical- 
ly removes you from more than 1,000 lists.

cerned about data theft or damage to your computer, you i 


should put the following security measures into effect. 


0 | HACK THYSELF 

The simplest way to know whether your PC has security holes 
is by taking on the role of hacker and attacking your own sys- 
tem. This is easier than it sounds if you click to Gibson Research 
Corp.’s SHIELDS uP! [grc.com]. Here an automated system will 
examine your Web address, scan for security problems, issue a 
detailed report on what’s wrong, and tell you what to do about 
it. Other useful scanners can be found at HACKERWHACKER 
[hackerwhacker.com] and DSL REPORTS [dslreports.com]. 


02 LEARN NOT TO SHARE 


Always turn off your computer's file-and printer-sharing settings. } 
Otherwise, Internet intruders may be able to view the contents } 
of your hard disk and send messages to your printer. If you have ! 
a home network and are running a proxy connection, you need : 


only shut off the settings on the PC that’s connected directly to 


Ifyou’re really paranoid, turn off yourcomputer when you're not 
: usingit. Most cable broadband systems reset your network address 
i when youshut down; any hacker who may have been probing your 
} original address will be unable to find the new one assigned to you. 


03 INVEST IN SOME PROTECTION 

| There are several security software products that can help shield 
; your home PC from digital trespassers. BLACKICE DEFENDER 
} [networkice.com] monitors and reports on computer intrusions 
| ($40). ZONEALARM [www.zonelabs.com] automatically plugs 
: exploitable security holes (free to home users). Also check out the 
H respectable NORTON INTERNET SECURITY 2000 [norton.com] ($60). 

If you have multiple computers linked over a home network, 
: you might want to consider investing in some hardware protec- 
ition. LINKSYS INSTANT BROADBAND ETHERFAST CABLE/DSLROUTER 
[www.linksys.com] connects to your cable or DSL modemand has 
four 10Base-T Ethernet ports for attaching computers with net-

TRYIT BY JIM LOUDERBACK 


It’s designed for use in the workplace, but the 
snazzy and affordable iPaq fits right in at home 


N APRIL, MY WIFE KIMMI’S FIVE-YEAR-OLD PC 
“Old Creaky,” finally drew its last breath. I 
tried my best to resuscitate the ancient sys- 
tem, but ultimately it had to be unplugged. } 
When it was tossed, Kimmi wept. She loved } 
the poor thing. 

After two weeks of mourning, Kimmi reluc- 
tantly agreed to search foranew home PC. Atfirst 
Ithought she mightlike one of those newfangled } 
Net appliances—stripped-down PCs that do | 
nothing but access the Web. But then I realized } 
that we needed a computer that could commune i 
with our Hewlett-Packard fax/printer. That ruled 
out most Net appliances, which generally 
support for computer peripherals. We also } 
ookedat Apple’s colorful iMac productline, but, H 
hey—we’re a Windows family. 
In the end, we decided to purchase an “Inter- 
net PC,” which is a type of computer optimized 
or Net use. Typically, standard versions of these 
systems offer a 500MHz Celeron processor, 64MB H 
of RAM, and a modest 4GB hard drive. 
Gateway, NEC, and Sony offer Internet PCs, ; 
but these oddly shaped systems include an expen- 
sive flat-panel LCD display. I wasn’t interested in 
paying platinum prices for copper capabiliti 
Besides, Old Creaky’s 17-inch ViewSonic moni 
tor was still in fine working condition. 

Dell offers something called the WebPC, area: 
sonably priced system (it starts at $679) with } 
solid computing power. But Kimmi couldn't } 
stand to look at it: She thought it resembled one i 
of Three Mile Island’s cooling towers. 

Compaq's comparable iPaq caught my wife's 
eye, however. This sleek silver-and-gray box } 
weighs a scant 10 pounds and starts at $499. It H 
runs Windows 2000 and ships without a moni- } 
tor, modem, or CD-ROM drive. 

Although the iPaq is designed to run on a net- 


work in the workplace—not in the home—we : 


felt the computer was priced just right. We paid i 


: immedi- 


: remove the iPaq’s CD-ROM drive. It slides in and 


‘ ithas two USB sockets built into the front panel, 


! fect for home use, and Compaq would be wise to 


ately pre- 
ferred it to 
Windows 
95 and 98; 
itmade set- 
ting upa 
home net- 
work a cinch 
and seemed to 
speed up our Net 
connection. It’s also 
quite stable. We've been 
using the iPaq for several 
months now, and Windows 
2000 hasn’t crashed yet. 

The computer has felt sluggish lately, and I've 
considered increasing the system’s memory from 
64MBs to 128MBs. With the iPaq, this is a simple 
task—just slide off the PC’s side panel and plug 
in new memory modules. It’s also effortless to 


out ofa bay in the front of the system and can be 
replaced with a Zip drive or a DVD player. 

The iPaq doesn’t have any expansion slots, how- 
ever. You can't adda graphics acceleration board | 
orasound card. It also isn’t powerful enough to 
play “blood-’n’-guts” computer games—but that 
isn’t a problem, because Kimmi isn’t much of a 
gamer. Instead, she uses the computer to sync up 
with her PalmPilot and to upload pics from our 
digital camera. The iPaq is ideal for these tasks; 


so you can easily plug in peripherals. 
Intruth, the only thing] dislike about ournew 
computer is its “business” label. The iPaq is per- 


introduce a consumer model. Kimmilovesit too. 
And Old Creaky? Well, it’s all but forgotten.

When we got the PC home, we managed 
to getit up andrunningin less than 10 min-

users or graphics designers, but it packs more 
than enough punch to handle all your Web surfing, 
e-mail, and word-processing needs.

T BEGAN AS A MODEST HOMAGE TO YOUR FAVORITE ACTOR, ACTRESS, 
author, sports team, musician, or hobby. Today, your Web site 
is a colossal work of code. In fact, it’s so large that parts of it 
are practically unnavigable. How can you improve it? Adda 
search function to yoursite. Any of the Web’s top hosted search 


searchable database of your site. 

The most popular hosted search engines include aTomMz.com, 
BESEEN MY SEARCH [mysearch.looksmart.com], FREEFIND 
[freefind.com], PICOSEARCH [www.picosearch.com], and 
SEARCHBUTTON.COM. How do they work? With your permission, 
these services scan your Web site and index its pages. They then 
e-mail youasnippetof HTML, which you paste into yoursite’s body 
of code. That's it. No software or hardware is required. 

Most of the hosted search services provide detailed reports that 
reveal what information your visitors have been requesting. Many 
also let you customize the design of the search tool and results 
page to reflect the style of your site. Atomz even lets you control 
which pages get returned for a particular query.

<<io3 Internet Madness 


that “60 percent of regular Internet users 
say the Internet has reduced their TV view- 
ing.” But as the study itself points out, it’s 
impossible to use WebTV and watch TV at 
the same time (they use the same device). 
No wonder, then, that the tube took a hit. 

And the Net, it turns out, is many 
things: e-mail, home pages, online games, 
pornography, chat, cams. The HomeNet 
testers treated this complex medium asa 
uniform agent. The guerrilla warfare ofa 
chat on AOL is an experience altogether 
different from e-mail with another Phish 
fan in the tapers’ conference on the WELL, 
or from your dissertation research. 

Relationships conducted online, argue 
pundits like Salon.com’s Scott Rosenberg, 
are no less real than relationships conduct- 
edin person, on the phone, or by letter. They 
can even be deeper and richer than the rela- 
tionships we have with our neighbors or 
pals in the next cube. But one thing’s for 
sure: They're different, and it’s misleading 
to compare flesh-and-blood relationships 
with electronic companionships. “When 
you try to define all fruit in its relation to 
apples,” writes Grohol in his critiques, post- 
ed at GROHOL.coo, “I think you're going to 
eventually come to the conclusion that an 
orange is very much lacking the quality 
characteristics of ‘appleness.’” 

Another general shortcoming of the 
studies is that, typically, completed 
research is validated through the scrutiny 
of peer review. The HomeNet study is the 
only one that underwentsuch scrutiny. “In 
the academic world, to go through peer 
review would have taken years,” says Nie. 
“By that point, the study would have strict- 
ly been for historical purposes.” He’s exact- 
ly right and exactly wrong—peer review 
legitimizes studies so that their findings 
can become part of the historical record. 

The birth of the concept of Internet addic- 
tion is even more absurd. The term itself 
comes from a parody “disorder” described 
in 1995 ona site dedicated to eating disor- 
ders, run by a doctor named Ivan Goldberg. 
The Internet addiction disorder included 
symptoms such as “obsessive thinking 
about what is happening on the Internet,” 
and “voluntary or involuntary typing 
movements of the fingers.” Some surfers 
took the spoof seriously, and Mental Health 
Net even posted Goldberg’s criteria. 

A year later, Young followed up with 
her study, which stated that “Internet 
addiction does exist.” Though some col- 
leagues dismissed that conclusion and 


questioned her methodology, the term 
“Internet addiction” took off. Young, 
author of Caught in the Net: How to Recog- 
nize the Signs of Internet Addiction—and a 
Winning Strategy for Recovery, went on to 
found the Center for On-Line Addiction, 
“the first consultation firm and Web-based 
resource network to specialize in Inter- 


THE MOST-FREQUENT USERS 
REPORTED SPENDING LESS 
TIME IN THEIR CAR, AT 
SHOPPING MALLS, AND 
WATCHING TV. 
COULDN'T THESE FINDINGS BE 
SPUN AS POSITIVE 
HEADLINES? THE 
INTERNET REDUCES GRIDLOCK.’ 
‘THE NET'S MORE FUN THAN TV!’ 


net-elated conditions such as problem 
day trading, online infidelity, virtual sex- 
ual offenders, and cybersexual addiction.” 

Although the Stanford, HomeNet, and 
Young studies are riddled with problems, 
they’re not entirely wrong. The Stanford 
study identified trends other than isolation. 
One-quarter of employed Internet users 
reported that it increased the time they 
spent working at home without decreasing 
their office hours. The most-frequent users 
reported spending less time in their car, at 
shopping malls, and watching TV. 
Couldn't these findings be spunas positive 
headlines as well? “The Internet Reduces 
Gridlock.” “The Net’s More Fun Than TV!” 

Both Rheingold and Rosenberg attacked 
HomeNet’s conclusions that people’s social 
contacts diminish the more time they spend 
on the Net, and they counter that people do 
connect socially online. But as the Net has 
become more corporate, what were once 
cozy electronic hangouts have turned into 
neon strips and stadium-sized shouting 
matches. “The virtual world can give play to 


the world of ideas and emotions, but it can 
create unrealistic expectations,” says Levin- 
son. “There are a whole set of disappoint- 
ments with online communities.” 

And Net addiction, as specious as it 
might be as a medical term, happens. A 
friend recently returned to New York after 
nine months in Idaho and stayed up until 
5 a.M. for weeks straight chatting with a 
Polish painter he was falling for. Now, that 
might be love. Butit might also bean emo- 
tional narcotic. 

Grohol calls online behaviors “phasic.” 
Most new Internet users go through a stage 
of intense curiosity, the same way that peo- 
ple might acclimate to a novel environ- 
ment by immersing themselves in it. But 
they move toward balance, Grohol writes, 
just “like a teenager learns not to spend 
hours on the telephone (eventually!).” The 
Polish girl goes back to her life; her suitor 
goes back to looking for an apartment. 

We might consider the history of atti- 
tudes toward new technologies phasic. 
Henry David Thoreau fretted that all the 
telegraph was going to do was stuff our head 
with nonsense like “Princess Adelaide has 
the whooping cough.” (Some would say he 
was right.) Levinson points out that at the 
turn of the century, movies were thought 
to turn impressionable young people into 
criminals. The printing press, the phone, 
and television had their detractors at their 
inception, and still do. The Internet is just 
the next in line. 

And the uproar continues. At press time, 
another New York Times report, this one by 
Jane Brody, citing Young among other 
cybersex “experts,” added more fuel to the 
Internet addiction fire. On the other hand, 
refuting the Internet isolation line,a study 
by the Pew Internet and American Life Pro- 
ject found that more than half of Net users 
reported strengthening their social ties as a 
result of being online. 

“These are just the very first pages in a 
long opus on how these machines will 
affect us,” adds David Shenk, author of Data 
Smog, which studies the darker conse- 
quences of the information revolution. “In 
25 years, we'll be able to look back and get 
some answers. But the technologies will 
also need a lot more studying, even then.” 

While we wait for the results, Dave 
Kansas, remember to call your mother. & 


AUSTIN BUNN Writes about culture and tech- 
nology for Brill’s Content, The New York 
Times Magazine, Feed, and Salon.
